Iranian parliament to debate foreign influence, Hormuz oversight bills
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:12:39 UTC
16:12:39 KST

Serdar Dincel
16 August 2026•Update: 16 August 2026
Iran's parliament is set to hold a virtual session on Sunday to review separate bills on countering foreign influence and overseeing the Strait of Hormuz, state-run broadcaster IRIB reported.
Members of the parliament's National Security Committee will examine a “Smart Management Plan” for the strait, the outlet said.
Iranian lawmakers will also review a bill aimed at combating international crimes, it added.
The report came after Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesman Esmaeil Baghaei said Saturday that Iran had reached an agreement with Oman on a shipping route map for the Strait of Hormuz following weeks of technical negotiations.
The talks are part of technical negotiations launched under an Iran-US memorandum of understanding, which tasked Tehran and Muscat with developing a new navigation framework for the Strait of Hormuz.
While Iran and Oman have continued discussions on shipping routes, Tehran has repeatedly said any decision to reopen the strategic waterway remains contingent on the US fulfilling its obligations under the memorandum.

IDF kills second Hezbollah commander in round of weekend strikes in southern Lebanon
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:06:16 UTC
16:06:16 KST

The IDF killed senior Hezbollah commander Abu Hassan Alaa during weekend strikes in the Deir ez-Zahrani area in southern Lebanon, the military announced on Sunday morning.
Alaa served as a commander in Hezbollah's Bader Unit and had carried out attacks on IDF soldiers operating in the region.
The military noted that its strikes came alongside those from over the weekend at Hezbollah's headquarters in the Ansar area, in which Ali Samir Al-Haj Hassan, a battalion commander in Hezbollah’s Radwan Force unit, was killed.
Both attacks came in response to the incident in which three IDF soldiers were seriously wounded over the weekend.
However, the IDF noted on Saturday that, at the time of the strike, Hassan’s family was with him inside the headquarters.
IDF, PMO says military was unaware of civilians in Hezbollah HQ during strike
“It should be emphasized that the family members were not the target of the strike,” the IDF wrote in a statement. “The strike was specifically directed at Hassan, who was a lawful target under international law."
"The terrorist used his family as human shields, hiding alongside them inside the military headquarters.”
The Prime Minister's Office said that it had been unaware that Hezbollah put civilians in the Hezbollah military compound.
“Only later did the IDF learn that Hezbollah deliberately put civilians in that military compound. Hezbollah is willing to do anything, including using its own civilians as human shields, to falsely accuse Israel of deliberately targeting civilians, which the IDF clearly did not.”
Shoshana Baker and Corinne Baum contributed to this report.

Patient held in isolation in Soroka Hospital tests negative for Ebola after returning from DRC
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:06:12 UTC
16:06:12 KST

The Health Ministry declared that a patient held in isolation tested negative for Ebola on Friday.
The patient was admitted to Soroka Medical Center after returning from the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) days earlier and developing a fever.
The Health Ministry urged the public to avoid travel to the DRC and said anyone returning from the DRC or Uganda, where the Ebola outbreak has spread, should self-isolate and call the Health Ministry hotline at *5400 if symptoms appear within 21 days of return.
The Health Ministry emphasized that currently there have been no confirmed cases of Ebola in Israel.
The current Ebola outbreak in the DRC is caused by the Bundibugyo species of Ebola, which does not have an approved cure or vaccine and is currently on track to be the largest outbreak in history, surpassing that of 2014-2016, in which 28,616 cases and 11,310 deaths were recorded across Guinea, Liberia and Sierra Leone, according to the World Health Organization.
Less than three months after the outbreak was declared, over 4,381 cases were confirmed and deaths had reached 2,011.
In 2014, it took a little over 12 months to reach the same number of deaths.
Ebola is not an airborne illness and can only be transmitted through direct contact with a sick person with symptoms or with blood, body fluids, or secretions.
The Health Ministry stated that it is continuing to monitor the situation and is conducting systemic preparations to ensure optimal preparedness of the health system for any possible scenario.
Reuters contributed to this report.

US to warn partners they must pick sides in artificial intelligence race with China
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:06:04 UTC
16:06:04 KST

The US is preparing to tell dozens of countries they must pick sides in the artificial intelligence race with China, warning they will be excluded from a US-led coalition if they also sign up for Beijing's competing framework, according to a US official and an internal draft reviewed by Reuters.
Washington last year launched the Pax Silica initiative aimed at securing supply chains for AI models, semiconductors and critical minerals, amid a fierce technology rivalry with Beijing.
About two dozen countries have joined, including Kazakhstan, a key potential source of critical minerals that has also joined China's coalition, as well as close US allies such as Japan, Australia, and South Korea.
The draft letter, prepared by the State Department, is addressed to the 35 signatories of a US "AI Opportunity Statement" signed in June, which includes members of the non-binding Pax Silica framework and other countries that have expressed a desire to align cooperation on AI with Washington.
By pressing countries to choose sides, the US hopes to starve China of resources in a race to make the most sophisticated AI, which could be used for military or economic dominance.
Jinping launches World Artificial Intelligence Cooperation Organization
In July, Chinese President Xi Jinping launched a rival "World Artificial Intelligence Cooperation Organization," promoting his country's open-weight technology as a challenge to US influence over the fast-moving sector.
Kazakhstan is the only country so far known to have joined both initiatives, setting off alarm bells in Washington.
"To be part of everything is to be part of nothing. Signature of the Pax Silica Declaration is not merely a membership subscription, but a commitment," the letter says, urging countries to "choose deliberately" on AI.
"It cannot be held alongside membership in duplicative initiatives whose expectations conflict with our own," the letter said, without specifically mentioning China.
Reuters could not determine when the US intends to send the letter or whether it might be amended before sending. The draft was undated.
The State Department told Reuters it would not comment on "purportedly leaked internal documents."
China’s embassy in Washington said the country opposes politicizing trade and technology issues. “Such actions will only stifle global AI advances and serve no one’s interests,” an embassy spokesperson said.
The Kazakh embassy in Washington did not respond to a request for comment.
The Pax Silica agreement aims to push US allies and partners toward joint projects and export controls, and ultimately reduce reliance on adversaries for critical minerals, AI models, and the semiconductor chips that power them.
The race between the US and China for technological leadership has reached a pivotal moment, as Chinese open-weight AI models have made rapid gains against proprietary systems from US companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic.
The exponential growth of the technology's capabilities, including the ability to hack autonomously, has forced a global reckoning over its power.
Beijing is weighing restrictions on overseas access to some of China's leading AI models, highlighting the growing tension with its stringent national security agenda.
The US touted Kazakhstan in June as the first country in Central Asia to join Pax Silica, bringing significant reserves of critical minerals that fuel advanced technologies.
China wielding critical mineral supply against Trump tariffs
China has used its current near-monopolies over critical minerals as a retaliatory weapon in a tariff war launched last year by US President Donald Trump, who has ramped up US efforts to source the minerals domestically and from allies.
Members of Pax Silica have access to shared investment opportunities in AI-related projects while those who sign the AI Opportunity Statement have symbolically agreed on a "common purpose" and "shared vision" with the US, according to the statement posted on the State Department's website.
US officials drafted the letter to make clear that "you can't have it both ways," the US official told Reuters, speaking on condition of anonymity given ongoing internal discussions on the issue.
"It's difficult to see how a country can credibly position themselves as trusted partners in one technology ecosystem while simultaneously signing up for an initiative designed by China to advance a competing vision for AI," the official said.

Hurricane Lala leaves thousands without power in Hawaii amid 'life-threatening' conditions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:05:51 UTC
16:05:51 KST

Hurricane Lala approached Hawaii with strong winds and heavy rain on Saturday, leaving more than 40,000 utility customers without power across the state as the National Hurricane Center warned of "life-threatening" conditions.
The poweroutage.com website showed 42,889 customers without power as of 2:14 p.m. HST in Hawaii, including 31,792 on the Big Island and 7,203 in Maui County, which includes the islands of Maui, Lanai and Molokai.
An advisory from the hurricane center at 2 p.m. HST predicted 10 to 20 inches (25.4 to 51 cm) of rain on the Big Island, and possibly up to 25 inches there, as well as 8 to 12 inches in eastern Maui and 4 to 6 inches in western Maui.
"This rainfall will produce life-threatening flooding and mudslides, especially in areas of steep terrain," the center said.
Hawaiian Electric warned customers to expect “possible extended outages, potentially overnight,” as the storm system made its way westward across Hawaii. On the Hamakua Coast on the Big Island, utility crews were assessing a 42-mile (67.6 km) span of transmission lines after fallen trees knocked down multiple poles and structures, Hawaiian Electric spokesperson Darren Pai told TV station Hawaii News Now.
Earlier in the day, the US National Hurricane Center said Lala's maximum sustained winds reached 75 mph (120 km/h), making it a Category 1 hurricane.
Winds of over 100 mph measured
The Maunakea Weather Center, on the Big Island's tallest peak, recorded wind gusts of more than 100 mph throughout the early afternoon, although winds would have been slower closer to sea level.
The Hawaii Department of Transportation said it was closing Hilo International Airport and would close Ellison Onizuka Kona International Airport at Keahole at 2 p.m. HST. The department also closed the Hawaii Island, Maui County, and Kaua‘i commercial ports.
The Environment and Climate Change portal is produced in cooperation with the Goldman Sonnenfeldt School of Sustainability and Climate Change at Ben-Gurion University of the Negev. The Jerusalem Post maintains all editorial decisions related to the content.

Two women killed in floods in Spain

Unknown Publisher

Two women killed in floods in Spain
MADRID, Aug 16 - A flood and landslide caused by torrential rain killed two elderly women and injured two babies in rural northwestern Spain, authorities said, as seven regions were placed on yellow alert for storms and heavy rain on Sunday.
The deaths of the women, aged about 60 and 80 years old respectively, were confirmed by emergency services of the Castile and Leon region after the disaster late on Saturday in the village of Manzanedo de Valdueza, near Pontevedra.
Two babies were treated for bruises, authorities added.
On Sunday, Spain's Civil Protection service warned of storms and heavy rain in seven regions of central and northern Spain.
The flooding forecasts follow weeks of record high temperatures that have led to a series of devastating wildfires. REUTERS

Could an Ethiopian Israeli become prime minister, president, or chief rabbi? - opinion
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:05:41 UTC
16:05:41 KST

An Ethiopian Israeli should be able to become the prime minister, the president, or the chief rabbi of Israel.
An Ethiopian Israeli should also be capable of leading a major Zionist or Jewish institution, and should appear on CNN, Fox News, the BBC, or Sky News.
Or even with Piers Morgan when discussing and debating Iran, Gaza, diplomacy, antisemitism, Jewish identity, and Israel’s future.
But until Israeli leaders, international Jewish organizations, and major media institutions begin treating Ethiopian Jews as potential leaders rather than permanent subjects of somebody else’s conversation, their language of diversity and inclusion will remain incomplete.
For decades, Ethiopian Israelis have been presented primarily through three narratives: immigration, discrimination, and poverty. Those stories matter. But they are not our entire story.
Where are the Ethiopian Israelis discussing national security, Jewish law, Africa, diplomacy, and Israel’s relationship with the United States?
Where are the Ethiopian Israeli candidates being prepared for cabinet ministries, senior diplomacy, major Jewish institutions, or national religious leadership?
International media organizations debate Israel every day, yet Ethiopian Israeli intellectual voices remain remarkably rare.
Israel also bears responsibility. Do not appoint an Ethiopian Israeli simply because an institution needs a Black face in a photograph.
And do not select an insufficiently prepared representative, watch that person struggle internationally, and then quietly conclude that Ethiopian Israelis cannot perform at the highest level.
Find the qualified people. We already exist.
Qualified Ethiopian Israelis
Today I present two leaders among many: Rabbi Reuven Wabashat and Fentay Maharat-Alamu. And, with humility, I include myself not as someone claiming entitlement to office, but as another example of a generation asking for the opportunity to serve.
Rabbi Reuven Wabashat represents a remarkable bridge between the ancient religious tradition of Ethiopian Jewry and contemporary Israeli rabbinical scholarship.
Born in Anbobar, Gondar, Ethiopia, he is a descendant of generations of Ethiopian Jewish spiritual leadership. His grandfather, Qes Wabashat Aytegb, and great-grandfather, Qes Aytegb Tamenu, were respected Ethiopian Jewish leaders.
He immigrated to Israel as a child in 1972, before the great Ethiopian aliyah operations. He later served as an IDF officer, became a rabbinical judge and halachic scholar, and authored works of Jewish law and biblical interpretation.
I know Rabbi Wabashat personally – as my rabbi and mentor.
So I ask a question Israel should confront seriously: Why should a scholar of his caliber be viewed principally as a rabbi for Ethiopians?
Why shouldn’t Ethiopian-Israeli Torah scholars compete for municipal chief rabbi positions – and eventually Israel’s highest rabbinical offices?
THEN THERE is Fentay Maharat-Alamu.
Her life challenges almost every stereotype about Ethiopian Israelis. Born in Gondar, Ethiopia, in 1984, Fentay was still a baby when her family undertook the perilous journey through Sudan toward Israel.
In the desert, the family ran out of water and came dangerously close to death.
During that ordeal, she suffered an injury near her eye. She arrived in Israel carrying a scar before she was old enough to understand what a scar meant. But the scar did not define the limits of her life.
Before entering Israel’s Foreign Service, Fentay accumulated roughly 15 years of professional and management experience in the private sector, including years at Rafael Advanced Defense Systems.
She later represented Israel diplomatically on several continents, including assignments in Chile, Ethiopia, and the Netherlands. Consider that transformation.
A one-year-old Jewish baby carried through the Sudanese desert grew up to represent the sovereign Jewish state internationally. That is not merely an Ethiopian story. That is a Zionist story.
Now Fentay is opening another chapter through literature and lectures. Her forthcoming novel, The Scarred Stone, received support from the Israeli center for Libraries even before publication.
The book turns the lens backward toward Beta Israel life in Ethiopia, particularly the lives, courage, and memories of its women.
Who were Ethiopian Jews before the world called us immigrants?
Before Sudan. Before rescue operations. Before absorption centers.
There were families, villages, traditions, religious scholars, mothers and fathers who dreamed of Jerusalem. We did not enter Jewish history when Ethiopian Jews landed at Ben-Gurion Airport. We were already living Jewish history.
Fentay’s forthcoming book therefore deserves an audience beyond the Ethiopian community. It offers readers an opportunity to encounter Ethiopian Jews not merely through rescue and suffering, but through family, faith, womanhood, memory, and belonging.
MY OWN path is different. I came through education, law enforcement, public service, journalism, and Jewish advocacy.
I studied educational leadership and conflict resolution, spent many years in American public service, and have written and spoken about Israel, Ethiopian Jewry, Jewish unity, moral diplomacy, equality, and world peace.
I mention this quietly because this article should not become my campaign biography. My point is, there are many of us.
Our community contains rabbis and diplomats, educators and officers, writers, entrepreneurs, academics, and public servants.
The tragedy is not that Ethiopian Israel has failed to produce leadership. It is that institutions and the stereotypical system too often fail to recognize it.
Political parties should recruit Ethiopian Israelis for meaningful parliamentary careers and prepare them for cabinet responsibility.
Religious institutions should cultivate Ethiopian Torah scholars for leadership of the entire Israeli public, not merely Ethiopian congregations.
The Foreign Ministry, universities, Jewish Federations, and international Zionist and antisemitism organizations should identify Ethiopian Israeli leaders and strategists capable of reaching their highest ranks.
International media must change as well.
We Ethiopian Israelis exist. Please call us to discuss Israel.
Can an Ethiopian Israeli become president of Israel? Why not?
Can an Ethiopian Israeli become chief rabbi? Why not?
Can an Ethiopian Israeli someday become prime minister of the Jewish state? Why not?
I’m not saying that Ethiopian Israeli leadership should replace anyone else, because leadership should be determined by knowledge, character, preparation, service, and vision.
Rabbi Reuven Wabashat represents one expression of that leadership. Fentay Maharat-Alamu represents another – from the dangers of Sudan to Israeli business, diplomacy, literature, and public service.
I humbly hope to contribute my own small part alongside them.
And we must start looking at those who are already contributing.

At least 10 injured, 12 killed after Polish bus overturned into ditch in Hungary
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:05:36 UTC
16:05:36 KST

A dozen people were killed and at least 10 injured after a Polish bus overturned at night on a motorway in eastern Hungary, Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar said on Sunday morning in a Facebook post.
The bus ran into a ditch and tipped over early on Sunday near the town of Mezokeresztes in eastern Hungary, police said, adding that the bus was heading towards the city of Nyíregyháza on the motorway when the accident happened.
State news agency MTI said there were 57 passengers and two drivers on the bus.
"A Polish-registered bus traveling on the M3 motorway towards Nyíregyháza... veered off the straight stretch of road into a ditch and overturned. Preliminary information suggests the driver likely fell asleep," police said on their website.
Twelve people lost their lives, and several others were injured in the accident, police said, adding that they were taking the bus driver into custody.
The injured have been taken to hospital.

The best person for the role - comment
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:05:35 UTC
16:05:35 KST

Justice Minister Yariv Levin has declared that if the Likud wins the Knesset elections, he will ask to continue in his post so that he can finally implement his judicial reform policy.
What needs reform even more is the size of the government. Admittedly, every party in a government coalition should be entitled to one minister, but regardless of the size of the party whose leader is the prime minister, no party, other than the ruling party, should have more than one minister in the government. The ruling party can have an extra minister in addition to the prime minister.
In delegating ministerial portfolios, the prime minister should forget about jobs for the boys and appoint the most suitable people from those who received the most votes in the primaries held by their respective political parties.
There were 12 ministers in the first government of Israel. The present government started with 30 ministers but now has 24. It’s not the largest government. There were two governments with 36 ministers. Naftali Bennett led Benjamin Netanyahu the other.
For political convenience, new portfolios are created, and some old portfolios are canceled or renamed.
In addition to the prime minister, ministries in the present government include: agriculture and food security; aliyah and integration; communications; construction and housing; culture and sports; defense; economy and industry; education; energy and infrastructure; environmental protection; finance; foreign affairs; health; heritage; innovation, science, and technology; interior; justice; national security; Negev, Galilee, and national resilience; religious services; social equality and the advancement of the status of women; tourism: transportation and road safety; and welfare and social affairs. In addition, numerous national authorities and other national bodies are engaged in formulating and determining policy.
By contrast, portfolios in Israel’s first government included agriculture, rationing and supply; education and culture; foreign affairs; finance, trade and industry; health; immigration and internal affairs; justice; labor and social security; police; religions and war victims; transportation; and welfare.
Issues further arise with the handling of these core ministries, such as education.
To the early governments of Israel, education was of primary importance after defense and diplomacy. In fact, a law on compulsory education was passed during the period of the first government.
But interest in education appears to be waning as teachers are made to work harder for less money and fewer vacation days.
There is almost always friction between official representatives of teachers’ unions and the Education Ministry, with threats and fears that some schools will not open at the beginning of September. This year is no different, but the situation is exacerbated by reports that teaching is no longer a desirable profession, and that there has been a sharp drop in the number of people opting for a career in education.
Education Minister Yoav Kisch has been quoted as saying that he loves the ministry and hopes to continue as minister if Likud wins the Knesset election.
But Kisch is not popular with teachers or their unions. If his wish comes true, more teachers will leave the profession. There is already an acute shortage of teachers. If their ranks continue to be depleted, what hope is there for Israel’s future?
Needed: An Israeli ministry for civil rights
In addition, though top-heavy with ministerial portfolios, some of which could either be merged or eliminated, Israel’s government desperately needs a ministry or authority for national priorities and the implementation of civil rights.
Israel’s founding fathers, namely the signatories to the Declaration of Independence, though coming from vastly different ideologies, agreed that “the State of Israel will ensure complete equality of social and political rights to all citizens, irrespective of religion, race or sex. It will guarantee freedom of religion, conscience, language, education and culture. It will safeguard the Holy Places of all religions…..”
There’s a missing word – “residents.” It’s a known fact that certain residents of Israel who wish to become citizens have had their applications denied year after year. Some who are non-Jewish, but were married to Israeli Jews and have Israeli-born children, were deported after divorce or widowhood.
Some Arabs whose families have lived in this country for generations are also denied citizenship and most of the civil rights that go with it.
How can we ever make peace with the Palestinians while continuing to discriminate against Arabs in our midst?
Some of the Arabs living in Israel are married to Arabs living in Gaza. Before October 7, the Gazan spouse used to come to Israel every few weeks for two or three days. This is no longer permitted, and families rely on social media platforms to stay connected. If the Gazan spouse had been coming to Israel for years and never violated any Israeli laws, is it not a violation of the civil rights of the partner living in Israel to keep the two of them apart?
When Soviet authorities imprisoned Natan Sharansky and kept him apart from his wife Avital for 12 years until they were reunited with the help of US president Ronald Reagan, US diplomacy, and a prisoner exchange, the mobilization of world Jewry for the whole period in which Sharansky was incarcerated kept the issue burning.
Gazan spouses barred from Israel don’t have that kind of support, though their plight is occasionally mentioned in left-wing publications and on social media.
Of course they cannot be compared with Sharansky, who was a dissident dedicated to exposing Soviet tyranny and facilitating the emigration of Soviet Jews. But the bottom line in both cases is the break-up of family.
Family break-ups among Israeli families have also occurred because the husband, a reservist, spent too long away from home while serving in the army and was not adequately compensated for the loss of income.
There has to be a ministry other than social welfare that concerns itself with humane and human rights issues.

Haymanot Kasau's parents demand equal resources after missing mother, daughter found in one week
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:05:32 UTC
16:05:32 KST

The family of Haymanot Kasau, who has been missing for more than 900 days, has renewed their push for a more extensive search into her disappearance following the discovery of Mali and Liel Yahalomi in Argentina only about a week after they disappeared in Vienna and following an extensive search operation by the Israel Police in Israel and abroad.
Haymanot's family is demanding that the same capabilities and resources used in the search for Mali and Liel also be employed in the investigation into her disappearance, and is calling for the case to be transferred to the Shin Bet (Israeli Security Agency).
A statement from the campaign headquarters seeking Haymanot's return home said that "With the mother and daughter having been found and Israel Police succeeding in solving the investigation, its helplessness in investigating Haymanot's abduction stands out even more, and therefore the family is demanding that the investigation be transferred to the Shin Bet."
Haymanot's parents, Benchi and Tesfaye Kasau, also responded to the discovery of the two women, saying that alongside their joy that the case ended safely, they could not help but compare the efforts invested in that search with the handling of their daughter's case.
"After Shabbat ended, we received the happy news that Mali and Liel Yahalomi had been found safe and well. Once again, a happy ending, except it is not our happy ending. Our Haymanot has not been with us for 902 days. 902 days have passed since our little daughter was abducted."
The parents added, "Haymanot was 9 years old; she did not choose to disappear, she did not choose not to come home, and despite this, not everything possible has been done to find her. Israel Police, under the direction of Inspector-General Danny Levy, used all of its capabilities to find Mali and Liel, but what have they done for our Haymanot? Lahav 433 has investigated half the world to find them, and they still don't meet with us or update us properly.
"We are once again asking Israel Police to transfer the investigation into Haymanot's abduction to the Shin Bet, and that Police Commissioner Inspector-General Danny Levy and Lahav 433 head Commissioner Meni Binyamin, who have failed to find any lead in the investigation, transfer the case to the Shin Bet because our girl is still not here."
In an appeal to the public, the parents said, "And to the dear people of Israel, whose citizens continue to show us the light amid all the darkness, and we understand that many of them, like us, have expressed their anger over the discrimination in the handling of the investigation into Haymanot's abduction, thank you. Thank you very much, and please, stay with us; help us make our cry heard because, unfortunately, no one listens to us."
Mother, daughter found in Argentina after planning disappearance
Mali and Liel Yahalomi captivated the country over the past week as the mystery surrounding their disappearance from the streets of Vienna unfolded. On Friday night, Israel Police announced that the two had been located on a bus in Argentina, safe and well. Police questioned them and released them after they said they had left of their own free will. Police clarified that they had no suspicion of criminal activity and had not opened an investigation.
Details revealed after they were found indicated that the two had planned their disappearance in advance, but had not taken into account law enforcement authorities' ability to trace digital footprints and piece together information collected in different countries.
"Israel Police, in cooperation with all security agencies and relevant authorities in Israel and abroad, operated in recent days in an extensive and focused effort to locate the missing women," the police stated.
"At the direction of Police Commissioner Inspector-General Danny Levy, the police, led by Lahav 433 and the Intelligence Division, operated command centers in Israel and abroad around the clock. According to the police, all necessary means and resources were allocated to the searches, hundreds of investigative actions were carried out, and cooperation took place with Europol and other police organizations around the world."
A police source told Maariv, "From the outset, we said that there apparently was no criminal incident or terrorist incident here, and that the mother and daughter had most likely left the room of their own free will. Representatives of the police Intelligence Division managed to track them down, and they were surprised when we reached them. After a brief questioning, and once we understood that there was no criminal or other motive, they were released. There is no suspicion that they committed an offense."
Haymanot Kasau's family marks 900 days since disappearance
Haymanot Kasau disappeared from an absorption center in Safed more than 900 days ago and has not been found since. Her family says police still have no leads in the case and has long demanded Shin Bet involvement.
Just this week, on Wednesday, family members, members of the campaign to bring Haymanot home, and activists demonstrated near Shin Bet offices in Tel Aviv, calling on Shin Bet chief David Zini to make the agency's intelligence capabilities available to the investigation.
During the protest, Haymanot's father, Tesfaye Kasau, read a personal letter he had written to Zini in Amharic, after which its contents were also read in Hebrew. "Tomorrow we will mark 900 days that our daughter has not been home, and my heart is struggling to bear the pain, the worry, and the helplessness," the father wrote.
The family argues that the investigation should also be transferred to the Shin Bet in light of the agency's involvement in other areas, including the fight against organized crime and weapons trafficking in Arab society and the search for the child Yuval Kogan. They say the agency's resources and intelligence capabilities should also be made available in the effort to locate Haymanot.
In his letter, the father also raised a painful allegation regarding the way the case has been handled. "I am doing everything in my power to stop myself from thinking that it has to do with color, but it is very difficult not to think about how things would have looked different if we looked different. If she were blonde, would her life matter more? If she were not a new immigrant, Ethiopian, would you already be helping us? Prove to us that this is not the case."

Top Iranian officials discuss country’s latest developments
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:02:48 UTC
16:02:48 KST

In the meeting, President Masoud Pezeshkian, Speaker of the Parliament Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf, and Judiciary Chief Gholamhossein Mohseni Ejei reviewed the latest developments facing the country and discussed the key ongoing issues and priorities across various sectors.
They also stressed the need to maintain coordination and synergy among the three branches and adopt coherent and coordinated approaches to advance the country’s affairs and safeguard the public interest.

Premier League fans’ previews: our club-by-club guide to 2026-27
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:00:20 UTC
16:00:20 KST

Arsenal
Having finally got the title monkey off our back, and with much of the competition in a state of flux, there’s plenty of optimism. Obviously the target is for Arteta to sustain the same appetite and intensity to mount a credible defence. The big questions are whether we can reproduce the same resilience at the back with Saliba on the long-term missing list, and will the introduction of Guimarães be the catalyst for the sort of midfield chemistry we’ll need to carve open opponents who plan to park the bus?
Key players/weak links On the evidence of our pre-season outings we urgently need the return of our remaining World Cup stars – Rice, Zubimendi and Saka should provide a renewed sense of purpose, both to counter any defensive fragility and to increase our goal threat. We’re all hoping for a breakthrough season for a beefed-up Max Dowman, and are anxious to discover if Tzolis is an upgrade on Trossard.
We still need to sign … Our friendly outings have shown the need for alternative attacking options, such as Atlético’s Julián Álvarez, and cover for Saliba – Quanseh and Konsa are linked. But presumably any further arrivals will be dependent on some astute pruning of those who’ve failed to cut the mustard, such as Vieira and Nelson.
Our cult hero is … Benny Blanco’s off-the-ball shenanigans ensure that he’s enshrined as a firm terrace favourite, but it’s the demeanour of Declan Rice that will invariably set the tone for whether or not we’re destined for more success.
If we could sign two players from our past … Who wouldn’t want to add the artistry of Thierry Henry and Dennis Bergkamp to any team?
We will finish 1st. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Chelsea; 3. Manchester City; 4. Liverpool. Bottom three: 18. Coventry; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Sergej Jakirovic.
- 
  Bernard Azulay onlinegooner.com; @GoonerN5
Aston Villa
It would be daft to not be optimistic when each year under Emery we’ve created great memories, regardless of the previous summer’s transfer merry-go-round. Top six and a cup run of any flavour would count as a good season, and it may be that our best moments are back-end weighted as we integrate some exciting new talent. I understand that Manzambi is the best thing to come out of Switzerland since the cuckoo clock, and any 22-year-old with 187 senior appearances (Garnacho) must be all right. Perversely, the financial regulations that are holding us back should prevent too much moaning if we don’t get off to a flyer, and having a ground with only three sides might even help us make VP a fortress. In all, I’m fired up for the new season.
Key players/weak links The senior pros all need to keep drinking the Emery Kool Aid, but why wouldn’t they? Konsa remains a colossus and the purple prose around McGinn is fully deserved. If Kamara plays 40 senior games and not his usual 25 it will improve our finishing spot by two. Normally naming a young player to watch in these pages guarantees them a loan move to Lincoln, but Brian Madjo has looked immense up front in pre-season.
We still need to sign … Marcus Rashford.
Our cult hero is … McGinn must already have done enough to have a hospitality suite named after him. Another two years and it could be a stand. He will be aware that Emery doesn’t deal in sentimentality so I expect yet another year in which SJM pushes for player of the season while being a key figure in the dressing room.
If we could sign two players from our past … Dwight Yorke, from 1995-96 when he switched to an out-and-out striker. And Gareth Barry: the complete midfielder.
We will finish 6th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester City; 3. Manchester United; 4. Chelsea. Bottom three: 18. Fulham; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Roberto De Zerbi.
- 
  Jonathan Pritchard
Bournemouth
We’re so looking forward to this: our first ever season in a European club competition. It’s going to be monumental. And, with arguably our strongest ever squad to call on, we expect to be competitive in the Europa League too. We’re excited to see Marco Rose in charge and a continuation of the fast, attacking, no-fear brand of football that earned us our highest ever finish last season. In view of the extra games we have on the schedule, a mid-table Premier League finish would be very respectable, but winning a trophy feels weirdly achievable. Why not a European one?
Key players/weak links António Silva has big Senesi-sized shoes to fill at centre-half but we expect big things from him. We’re also looking forward to the impact that new striker Álvaro Rodríguez and the attacking right-back Juanlu will bring. Watch out too for a fully fit Ben Gannon-Doak – a breakthrough season beckons for him.
We still need to sign … Business is pretty much done. We’re in a good place, with maybe one or two to go out on loan deals towards the end of the window.
Our cult hero is … Long-serving veteran defender Adam Smith continues to perform at the highest level, an incredible 16 years after his debut for the club. He epitomises the journey we’ve been on and we love him for it.
If we could sign two players from our past … It would be hard to turn down a peak Nathan Aké or Callum Wilson, but then with the squad that we have now, I’m not convinced they would get into a starting 11. Perhaps, with Junior Kroupi out for a few months, there might be a place for a natural finisher like Jermain Defoe. Better still, prime Ted MacDougall would score hatfuls every week in this team.
We will finish 12th. Top four: 1. Manchester City; 2. Arsenal; 3. Liverpool; 4. Manchester United. Bottom three: 18. Hull; 19. Ipswich; 20. Leeds. First manager sacked Xabi Alonso.
- 
  Jeff Hayward Back of the Net, the AFC Bournemouth Fan’s Podcast
Brentford
I’m very optimistic. Twelve months ago we had Yoane Wissa refusing to train to force a move and the footballing world was convinced that our free-kick coach turned manager would get us relegated. Fast forward a year and things feel very different. We’ve had a proper pre-season, there’s a clear tactical approach and, importantly, we’ve signed players who can actually execute it. The target? Keep improving. If we can get a European spot this season, that would be another sign that we’re moving forward year-on-year. For a club like Brentford, that’s a pretty exciting prospect.
Key players/weak links There’s a lot of excitement around new signing Mamadou Sangaré – so much so that he’s already got his own song. Callum Wilson is a shrewd signing too, and, at this stage of his career, seems happy to sit on the bench and knock a few in when called upon. Our explosive front three of Ouattara, Thiago and Schade have now had time to gel, and should cause plenty of problems. As for youngsters, 11 players aged 21 or under are vying for a place in the final 25-man squad. 19-year-old striker – and rapper – Kaye Furo is highly rated, as is 20-year-old centre-back Jannik Schuster. It’s a good sign that we’re getting to the point where our youngsters aren’t just making up the numbers
We still need to sign … If there’s one position we could strengthen, left-back would be a good option. El Hadji Malick Diouf from West Ham has been rumoured.
Our cult hero is … Michael Kayode. Cemented his status by singing his own terrace chant on camera: “He eats spaghetti, he drinks Moretti, he hates Queens Park Rangers.”
If we could sign two players from our past … We’re probably a bit too “nice”. Whenever we need a short injection of old-school fire, fight and chaos, Terry Evans from our 1992 Division One-winning side and Terry Hurlock from that incredible 80s “Kamara, Bowles, Hurlock” midfield would be great to call upon.
We will finish 6th. Top four: 1. Manchester City; 2. Arsenal; 3. Manchester United; 4. Bournemouth. Bottom three: 18. Fulham; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked I got it spot-on last season, predicting two consecutive Forest sackings. Surely Glasner isn’t next on the cards? OK, go on then …
- 
  Billy Grant Beesotted podcast and blog; @Beesotted; @BillyTheBee99
Brighton
Having seen West Ham, Chelsea and even Crystal Palace go all the way in the Conference League, the main target is doing well in that and winning a first cup final since the 1910 Charity Shield. Am I optimistic about it happening? Not really. The competition looks tougher this year. Freiburg, Atalanta and Monaco all have proper European experience. Even the playoff round against Tromsø is a banana skin. I’m old enough to remember some pretty horrible trips to Carlisle when Brighton could never win north of Lake Windermere. Let alone inside the actual Arctic Circle.
Key players/weak links The key player will be whoever Fabian Hürzeler uses to fill the void left by selling Danny Welbeck: Georginio Rutter, Charalampos Kostoulas or Stefanos Tzimas. Not only did Welbeck score 13 goals last season, but most were vital equalisers or important winners worth 13 points. Allowing him to join Chelsea is a huge risk. If none of those younger alternatives who the club want playing more this season step up and replace Welbeck, there’s the weak link. And there are big question marks over all three.
We still need to sign … Danny Welbeck from Chelsea would be a good signing for his goals record and the experience he would bring to a young squad.
Our cult hero is … Rutter. Nothing says cult hero quite like having one Instagram account about your football career and another about the racehorses you own. With the racing one being more interesting.
If we could sign two players from our past … I’ll spare you another Welbeck answer and say Glenn Murray and Bobby Zamora instead. Hopefully, I’ll be able to revisit this on 3 June 2027 after we’ve just won the Conference League and Kostoulas has hit 46 goals in all competitions. Danny who?
We will finish 13th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester United; 3. Manchester City; 4. Chelsea; Bottom three: 18. Sunderland; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Oliver Glasner.
- 
  Scott McCarthy WeAreBrighton.com
Chelsea
A new top manager, some decent new signings and a feelgood factor within the squad – it’s hard not to be optimistic. We’ve finally brought some experienced players who should complement our younger players and take them to the next level. With no Champions League football, the target has to be top four and a good run in the domestic cups.
Key players/weak links We’ve strengthened in most areas so it’s key that the new signings make a quick impact. Palmer and Colwill will hopefully have fully recovered, too, and will hit the floor running. Really looking forward to seeing Morgan Rogers in a blue shirt and Marco Palestra looks promising. I still worry with Sánchez in goal, though, and Penders might be a bit green for the Premier League. As for youngsters, I’d add Reggie Watson to the squad as a wildcard. He impressed in pre-season, giving off real John Terry vibes. How is he only 16?
We still need to sign … If Enzo Fernández goes he’ll need replacing; Alex Scott and Adam Wharton have both been heavily linked and either would be a good choice, adding to the English spine of the team. The rest of the squad looks very balanced with strength in depth. At this stage of the window the main focus will be on trimming the squad to a manageable level.
Our cult hero is … In a team with few real characters I’m hoping some of the new signings will shake the squad up a bit. I’ve a feeling Pep Chavarria could quickly become a fan favourite with some dark arts. Cole Palmer will continue to entertain on and off the pitch with his great interviews.
If we could sign two players from our past … John Terry to marshal the defence and Frank Lampard to bang the goals in. Both players gave 100% every time they stepped on the pitch and were also fantastic motivators. Two absolute legends who would grace any team in any era.
We will finish Fourth. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester United; 3. Manchester City; 4. Chelsea. Bottom three: 18. Newcastle; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Sergej Jakirovic.
- 
  Paul Baker in memory of Trizia Fiorellino
Coventry
There’s no downside to choosing optimism, so yes, we’re optimistic. I don’t expect a top-half finish but I do enjoy how the club’s being run and believe in our ability to compete. Clearly the target is to survive, but within that, if we can showcase what’s good about us and provide a challenge, that would be a very acceptable return.
Key players/weak links The money we’ve spent is basically unfathomable, but my focus has been on those who were so critical to our promotion. Matt Grimes connects everyone in the team so we’re relying on him doing that again, else we’ll have to develop a completely new way to function. Signing keeper Carl Rushworth also gives assurance at the back that we hope will translate into points. There are questions about our strikers (which feels barmy for a team that scored 97 goals last season). It’s not complicated – we simply don’t know if Wright and Simms have what it takes to score at the highest level. Aside from signing Aurèle Amenda, we haven’t touched our defence either. It’s a bold strategy. Liam Kitching and Jay Dasilva have improved significantly, but it’s hard to ignore the notion that we’re relying on players like this finding another uptick. However, new signing Caleb Yirenkyi looks very special.
We still need to sign … We saw how vital Brian Brobbey was for Sunderland last season. A dynamic, high-status striker would go a long way towards calming the nerves about our forward line. Is Taiwo Awoniyi able to be that guy? Let’s hope so. The rumours about Fikayo Tomori are exciting. If we could bring him in, good lord.
Our cult hero is … While the returning Gus Hamer is clearly our cult hero, Milan van Ewijk is the joker of the team. You’ll find him front and centre of every celebration. But don’t let the daftness fool you; he’s a serious, serious player.
If we could sign two players from our past … The original version of James Maddison was unlike anything I’ve ever seen. His game has adapted with age, which is a shame, but I’d love some of that early ingenuity. I’d also welcome back Viktor Gyökeres. He functions far better when he believes he’s the star man. Come home Vik and bash your way to upwards of nine Premier League goals for Coventry.
We will finish 17th. Top four: 1. Manchester City, 2. Chelsea, 3. Arsenal, 4. Manchester United. Bottom three: 18. Ipswich, 19. Fulham, 20. Hull. First manager sacked Daniel Farke.
- 
  Neil Allison @skybluesblog, Nii Lamptey Show Podcast
Crystal Palace
What are we expecting? Right now, it’s really difficult to say. The only key player to have exited (so far) is Maxence Lacroix, on whom we made a massive profit, but we still have to strengthen the squad so that it can cope with the rigours of going deep in the Europa League without risking our Premier League campaign – a lesson learned from last season. Lots of incoming deals seem close, but not yet over the line.
Key players/weak links Ismaïla Sarr and J-P Mateta will once again be essential, given the lack of goals in the rest of the squad. Adam Wharton will show how insane it was for Tuchel to leave him out of the World Cup, and Tyrick Mitchell will continue to excel on the left. Jaydee Canvot, still only 20, is destined to be a superb centre-back and should grow further in stature this season.
We still need to sign … There’s been some solid business already – US teenager Zavier Gozo and right-back Anan Khalaili look exciting prospects, and Evann Guessand is welcome back from Villa. But we still need a replacement for Lacroix, and strength in the middle – we had a £35m bid for Monaco’s Lamine Camara rejected. Plenty still to do. Meanwhile, the decision to swap Dwight McNeil for Brennan Johnson has split opinion: Johnson – all £35m of him – was only here a few months.
Our cult hero is … A few contenders. Mateta, obviously, chant-leader Dean Henderson and, potentially, our Brazilian attacker Matheus França. He’s not there yet, but he joined the club three years ago at 19 and has barely made an impact for various reasons. But he’s had a strong pre-season, including dancing goal celebrations – some Brazilian flair would go down very well at Selhurst.
If we could sign two players from our past … Michael Olise without a doubt; he was clearly a world-class player at Palace and has continued to progress spectacularly. And Matt Jansen, from the late 90s – a personal hero of mine; such incredible talent at such a young age and would shine in the modern era.
We will finish 14th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester City; 3. Chelsea; 4. Tottenham. Bottom three: 18. Newcastle; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull; First manager sacked Matthias Jaissle.
- 
  Chris Waters @Clapham_Grand
Everton
Despite the £327m the Friedkins have provided since the takeover, there are still doubts about the scale of their ambition. Recruitment is tough, the process (Moyes or committee as decision makers) is uncertain. Realistically, aiming for eighth (and European qualification) is the likely limit. Does that make Blues feel optimistic? Unlikely. The 31-year wait for a trophy hangs heavy on our hearts and minds.
Key players/weak links Midfield is looking stronger but can Beto/Barry score 20 goals between them? Tyrique George (acquired from Chelsea) is a key signing. The right side of the pitch remains problematic. A couple of youngsters to watch: Harrison Armstrong, 19, made his breakthrough last season, and, even younger at 18, Braiden Graham, the Northern Ireland forward signed from Linfield.
We still need to sign … A right-back. Jake O’Brien, who has been covering there, is a fabulous centre-back and would be perfect in the middle alongside Branthwaite. A proven goalscorer would be a bonus, as would a right-winger.
Our cult hero is … Jordan Pickford. His contribution to Everton maintaining top-flight status and his excellence in goal for England are shamefully under-reported in the media. We are blessed to have such a dedicated player who loves playing for his country, the club and his fans.
If we could sign two players from our past … Alan Ball, the best outfield player to play for the club in my opinion, never stopped running, commanded midfield, was skilful and a great passer and had a tremendous shot – he had it all, and would walk into any Premier League side of any generation. What would he be worth today? And a 17-year-old Wayne Rooney.
We will finish I said eighth last season, the optimist says eighth again. But realistically, 10th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Liverpool; 3. Manchester United; 4. Aston Villa. Bottom three: 18. Tottenham; 19. Coventry; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Oliver Glasner, not because he deserves it, but because of the owner.
- 
  Paul Quinn @theesk, theesk.org
Fulham
I’m always optimistic at this stage of the summer – but there’s a bit of trepidation mixed in this time round. How on earth do you make an informed judgment on our chances when so much has changed? It’s farewell (and thanks) to Marco Silva and a warm welcome to Álvaro Arbeloa, who brings with him a new set of backroom staff, a fresh approach and a determination to write a new chapter. Seems we’re set for a new, dynamic strategy, one that’ll keep us on the edge of our seats. In a good way, hopefully.
Key players/weak links We expect striker Gonzalo García (22) and versatile midfielder César Palacios (21) to hit the ground running. The Spanish duo have followed their former boss from Real Madrid knowing that running hard is part of the deal. Another midfielder, Shea Charles (22), also joined the fold, and, as well as helping to lower the average age of the squad, the trio will be eager to discard their “promising” tags by excelling in the top flight. Arbeloa may well turn to the youth ranks too, with 18-year-old winger Macaulay Zepa one to watch.
We still need to sign … If Arbeloa gets his way there’ll be further additions, probably to freshen up the back four. And maybe a younger keeper who’s good with his feet to take over from Leno.
Our cult hero is … For humour, the Naija Boys – Iwobi and Bassey – who will no doubt continue their off-pitch antics and comedic Instagram stories, although they’re a man light now that Chukwueze has headed back to Milan. Unless someone else steps up, captain Tom Cairney remains the main man, still capable of scoring match-winning worldies after parking his antique bath chair at the side of the pitch.
If we could sign two players from our past … Obvious goal-getter up front would be Louis Saha. And the super-strong Mousa Dembélé bossing things in central midfield would suit AA’s style perfectly.
We will finish 14th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester City; 3. Manchester United; 4. Chelsea. Bottom three: 18. Coventry; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Matthias Jaissle.
- 
  David Lloyd @DMLTOOFIF
Hull
I’m very optimistic. Sure, chances are that we’ll get relegated but we’ll certainly not be the pushovers people are expecting us to be. We thrive when we’re being written off, as last season showed, and have a pragmatic manager who has us playing brilliant, transitional football which suits the Premier League. We could definitely upset a lot of teams and maybe, just maybe, even stay up.
Key players/weak links As the squad currently stands, Oli McBurnie, Mohamed Belloumi and Charlie Hughes will be the key faces. But with new signings arriving to add some much-needed experience and necessary skill, that may change. Hidemasa Morita has been an absolute coup on a free, so he may be one to keep an eye on. Full-back is still a problem position for us: there’s only Ryan Giles on the left and questions over Lewie Coyle and Cody Drameh at this level on the right. Thanks to a late flurry in the window we’ve now got a healthy plethora of young talent hoping to break through. 18-year-old Australian Lucas Herrington comes as a fully fledged international and had the likes of Barcelona sniffing around before signing for us.
We still need to sign … We still really need depth in the full back areas and desperately need a forward or two to compete with McBurnie. We could do with some more Premier League experience across the back line, too, as well as another back-up goalkeeper thanks to Jack Butland’s lengthy injury.
Our cult hero is … Definitely McBurnie. He’s a handful for every defender and a constant wind-up merchant. The kind of player you hate to be against but love to have in your team.
If we could sign two players from our past … I would have Jarrod Bowen on the wing and Andy Robertson in at left-back. Strangely, they were two transfers I thought might actually happen this summer following West Ham’s relegation and Robertson leaving Liverpool on a free. Alas, it wasn’t to be.
We will finish 17th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Man City; 3. Liverpool; 4. Man United. Bottom three: 18. Crystal Palace; 19. Ipswich; 20. Coventry. First manager sacked Frank Lampard.
- 
  Ant Northgraves To Hull & Back Podcast
Ipswich
Having endured the Premier League two years ago, the fans know what to expect: eyes are well and truly open. The momentum and hope we had two seasons ago was stopped in its tracks pretty swiftly once the season started. This time round, the summer’s transfer business looks of a better quality, more robust, a step up in quality, giving us half a chance of survival and a smidge of optimism.
Key players/weak links Sasa Lukic looks to be exactly the type of signing we need to be making. 30 years old, good Premier League experience to add some no-nonsense mettle and physicality. There will need to be more like him and Issa Diop through the spine of the team. One youngster to watch: Sindre Walle Egeli has just turned 20, and signed for a Championship record fee of £17.5m last August. He had an up-and-down season but has had really good pre-season in the No 10 role.
We still need to sign … A senior striker, to take the pressure off our new, young Brazilian forward Emersonn, who was Gary O’Neil’s first signing when he joined from Toulouse for £25m in July. We were strongly linked with Callum Wilson before he went to Brentford. I do wonder whether we’ll get to the end of August and land Zian Flemming from Burnley.
Our cult hero is … Of the current players, Marcelino Núñez is probably the closest. He has plenty of credit in the bank after his transfer, last summer, from a lower league club. That said, Florentino Luís’s early social media posts mean he has serious potential in this category. We’ve already had AI farmers and Suffolk Punches on Insta & TikTok …
If we could sign two players from our past … Paul Mariner for his all-round presence at the top of the pitch. He would be ideal as the central striker in the modern game. And Matt Holland as a leader and box-to-box midfield general.
We will finish 16th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester City; 3. Manchester United; 4. Chelsea. Bottom three: 18. Fulham; 19. Coventry; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Álvaro Arbeloa.
- 
  Craig Finbow @BlueMondayITFC; bluemondayitfc.co.uk
Leeds
At the end of last season Daniel Farke challenged the owner, 49ers Enterprises, to move on from just surviving in the Premier League. So this summer Leeds have broken our transfer record for goalkeeper James “next Nigel Martyn” Trafford, paid £34m for defender Tarik Muharemovic, and gone back for Harry Wilson, one of last season’s top creators in the Premier League. Over to you, Daniel. His challenge now is turning ambition into results fast enough to prove he’s the manager to move Leeds on from just surviving. A long-lusted-for new West Stand is under construction at Elland Road and the mood is good, which can be dangerous for a self-professed massive club in the bottom half of the Premier League.
Key players/weak links The main strength, and weakness, is Dominic Calvert-Lewin. He’s our key goalscorer, but doesn’t have much back-up. Left-back is similar, but Jayden Lienou is a promising teenage understudy for Gabriel Gudmundsson.
We still need to sign … The priority is a striker to help Calvert-Lewin and Lukas Nmecha. There are rumours, though, about adding more flair for the forward line.
Our cult hero is … Anton Stach’s YouTube channel is, thankfully, a delightful contribution to happiness in our world. He’s been wandering the streets of Leeds incognito asking people what they have for breakfast, and filming himself buying EU plug adaptors in Boots. On Instagram, his partner’s gameday graphics have brought Sharknados to Elland Road. Good, wholesome stuff.
If we could sign two players from our past … The correct back-up for DCL would be Luciano Becchio. To add some suaveness to our bustling midfield, Gary McAllister.
We will finish 12th. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Aston Villa; 3. Liverpool; 4. Manchester City. Bottom three: 18. Sunderland; 19. Hull; 20. Coventry. First manager sacked Frank Lampard.
- 
  Daniel Chapman leedsista.com, @leedsista.com
Liverpool
I’m always optimistic about a new season, but this one feels very much like a new beginning for us and a real unknown. We start minus a few familiar faces alongside a new manager and some new signings, but that’s balanced with Van Dijk and Alisson still being here and others who’ve been at the club a while now. I’d put little store by the pre-season results or that Iraola said we can’t run after 60 minutes – he didn’t mean it – and we’ll see how it goes when it all kicks off for real next week. We have top players, the boss comes with a style that fits in with the club and I believe we’ll do well. The target is to be up there fighting for a top spot next May.
Key players/weak links At the back with all their experience, Van Dijk and Alisson will be crucial. There’s a big gap to fill up front, but Isak now has the chance to stake his claim after a nightmare season for him last time around. Wirtz too will want to show he can adapt to the pace of the Premier League and be a force. As for youngsters, Rio Ngumoha has already shown what he can do and the more he plays the better he will become. Trey Nyoni is pulling in the plaudits for his midfield play and if Giovanni Leoni continues his recovery he too has the potential to nail down a place.
We still need to sign … Defenders. Despite bringing in a couple, including Ifeanyi Ndukwe who won’t count yet, it still feels as if we need a solid centre-back and right-back for the immediate and long-term future. Konsa or Murillo at Forest? A central midfielder to help out Gravenberch and an attacker who plays on the right – Ibrahim Mbaye has been mooted, though PSG might balk at the idea of us coming after two players, because Barcola is a done deal. Apparently.
Our cult hero is … Not sure we have one at the minute, we need someone to step up.
If we could sign two players from our past … Alan Hansen to partner Van Dijk, and Steven Gerrard to inspire and work his magic from midfield all over the pitch.
We will finish 1st. Top four: 1. Liverpool; 2. Arsenal; 3. Chelsea; 4. Manchester City. Bottom three: 18. Nottingham Forest; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Pierre Sage.
- 
  Steph Jones
Manchester City
I’m excited; it’s a new dawn under Maresca. Naturally, every City fan loved Pep’s reign. We know we’ll never have it as good again. And I doubt we’ll ever see a more dominant managerial era in the Premier League. But after 10 years, naturally, there’s something exciting about a fresh start. The target is simple: we need to win the league back. The last team that weren’t City to retain the title were United in 2008-09. It’s our job to make it incredibly difficult for Arsenal.
Key players/weak links To temper the optimism slightly, losing Rodri, if it happens, is obviously a massive blow. He’s the best No 6 in the world, without doubt. So by definition, he’s irreplaceable. Having said that, given his age (30), the club were always planning to sign his successor this summer. And in adding Anderson and Bouaddi, it feels like Viana has done excellent business. The key players will be Haaland, Semenyo, Gvardiol, Donnarumma, O’Reilly, Cherki, Foden (if he can recover his level) and Doku. They’re the most nailed-on starters (plus Anderson). Youngster to watch? Ryan McAidoo.
We still need to sign … We’re still lacking depth out wide. McAidoo looks really promising. But if Doku or Semenyo pick up an injury, then currently (pending the Spurs interest) we’d have to rely on Savinho. He’d burnt most City fans’ heads out last season … so much natural talent, but an infuriating lack of end product. Hopefully we upgrade him if he goes. Our cult hero is … Has to be Haaland, doesn’t it? The bloke’s dripping in aura. The manner in which he schooled Gabriel v Brazil at the World Cup … delicious. More of that please.

We will finish 2nd. Top four: 1. Arsenal; 2. Manchester City; 3. Chelsea; 4. Tottenham. Bottom three: 18. Fulham; 19. Ipswich; 20. Hull. First manager sacked Sergej Jakirovic.

‘Our children are less cognitively capable,’ says neuroscientist who wants to cut screen use in schools
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:00:20 UTC
16:00:20 KST

Jared Cooney Horvath is on a mission. In the wake of global campaigns for smartphone bans in schools and social media restrictions for under-16s, the neuroscientist wants to raise the alarm about educational technology in classrooms.
A former teacher, Horvath believes the use of screens and digital tools in schools is undermining how children learn and develop. “This might be one of the hardest truths our generation has to face,” he writes in his book, The Digital Delusion.
“Our children are less cognitively capable than we were at their age,” he says. “For nearly two centuries, the west experienced steady generational progress. Each new cohort of children grew up, on average, healthier, happier, and better educated than the last. Until now.”
Starting around the year 2000, something changed. “Across the west, IQ scores began to fall – even as time spent in school kept rising,” writes Horvath. Scores in international tests started to drop. Why? “The answer lies in the meteoric rise of educational technology.”
According to a recent Department for Education report, the UK educational technology – or edtech – market generated an annual turnover of about £6.5bn. In the US, Horvath says it is a $400bn (£295bn) mega-industry, woven into every aspect of schooling.
“We’ve been making some incredible strides on the screen-based childhood stuff which even two years ago, I did not think was going to happen as fast as it did,” he said. “But it’s not just about getting rid of screens for entertainment. It’s about reducing them as much as possible for academic learning as well if we want our kids to have deep transferrable knowledge in our heads.”
The uptake of laptops in schools – especially in the US where he lives – began in earnest in 2010-12, Horvath said. “It’s when Covid hit, that’s when every school had to pivot digital as a Band-Aid … rightly so. Better to have something than nothing.
“But when Covid finished you would assume they’d rip that Band-Aid off, but the vast majority of schools didn’t. They just stayed digital.” In the US 88% of school districts now had one-to-one computers, he said. In the UK, Covid had a similar impact.
Horvath has been studying how people learn for over 15 years and has concluded that “tech is not a very good tool for it”. He cites recent scores from the programme for international student assessment (Pisa) which takes place every three years and tracks the academic performance of 15-year-olds across dozens of countries.
In 2012, 2015 and 2018, Pisa asked students how much time they spent using digital devices during a typical school day. “The more time students spent on screens at school, the further their scores fell,” Horvath said. “On average, those who used computers for more than six hours per day scored 66 points lower than their peers who didn’t use them at all.”
Others working in the field are concerned about the risks of confusing correlation with causation. Rose Luckin is an emerita professor of learner-centred design at the UCL Knowledge Lab in London and a recognised expert on AI in education.
“[Horvath] does get certain things right,” Luckin said. “It’s true that a great deal of educational technology was sold on promises rather than proof. We did not have a huge amount of evidence. But that does not mean that all educational technology is problematic.
“In terms of that Pisa data, if you look at it on computer use and outcomes, actually moderate school device use is associated with better results than no school device use. It’s just that the heaviest daily use is the worst.”
Daisy Christodoulou, the director of education at No More Marking, an educational technology startup, agrees that society has been a “bit blase” about the impact of a lot of these new technologies.
“We are seeing across the board now a bit of a corrective, where people are realising that. But I would probably be a little bit more optimistic [than Horvath] about the potential for technology and AI in upper secondary,” she said. “Screens have not had as big an impact in classrooms in England as in the US. But I feel like there’s enough here that we should be thinking about it. I think it’s a bit blase to go, ‘nothing to see here’, move on please’.”
Dylan Wiliam, an emeritus professor of educational assessment at the UCL Institute of Education, said: “Technology may well be harming student achievement but the correlational studies that we currently have cannot support such causal claims.
“And there are many well-designed studies that show that technology can have a positive impact on student achievement. So the question, ‘Does edtech have a negative impact on learning?’ does not have a simple yes/no answer: sometimes it does, and sometimes it doesn’t.
“The question we need to be asking is, ‘when does edtech help learning?’ And the answer is, ‘it’s complicated’.”

AI cheating, leaked papers and marking errors: how exam protests went global
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:00:19 UTC
16:00:19 KST

Families with teenagers in education know the private, hidden pain of exam season. But this year, what might have been a summer of quiet family anxiety has erupted in several countries into public unrest.
Exam-related turmoil has led to mass student protests in Mexico after nearly 60,000 university applicants were forced to resit tests amid suspected cheating, while Portugal’s disastrous attempt to digitise school exam marking sparked the country’s worst education crisis in decades.
By far the largest and most ground-shaking incident was in India, however, where an exam paper leak affected millions of students and was linked to more than a dozen students taking their own lives. The scandal forced the education minister to resign, and challenged the government’s hold over its 1.4 billion citizens.
Why does this exam season seem to be particularly tense and volatile? Experts say the growth of AI cheating and attempts by academic institutions to digitise examinations are at the heart of the issue. When coupled with the high stakes of good grades in an increasingly precarious global economy, the result can be explosive.
“It would seem that exams are getting more fraught on a global scale,” said Dr Sarah Eaton of the Werklund School of Education at the University of Calgary. “The pressure on young people has absolutely changed.
“We’ve seen the economic situation change around the world in the last decade. And the results of one examination determining a person’s entire future really creates untenable pressure on young people,” said Eaton, a leading expert on academic ethics. “This is a tragedy when young people are dying by suicide because of an examination outcome.”
When a single test can determine a young person’s entire future, cheating is no longer just about individuals but becomes a systematic issue, said Eaton. This is compounded when illicit commercial industries, known as the “education mafia”, exploit student desperation by stealing and then selling exam papers, something that has become widespread in India and elsewhere.
Students from across India joined the mass demonstrations led by the Cockroach Janta party, a youth-led protest campaign that began as an online satirical joke and grew into a nationwide outcry that poses an unprecedented challenge to Narendra Modi’s government.
“When you get large-scale fraud combined with systemic failure, there’s almost a guarantee of a breakdown of public trust,” said Eaton. “And once that trust is broken, it’s almost impossible to regain. I can’t imagine how India is going to deal with this, just given the levels of corruption and fraud that we’ve seen.”
In Portugal, the national fallout from a failed push by its education minister, Fernando Alexandre, to fully digitise the exam marking process has landed the government in a crisis. Parents, teachers, and students have been protesting, demanding Alexandre’s resignation after his policy resulted in serious marking errors.
Similar demonstrations took place in Mexico. The National Autonomous University of Mexico (Unam), the country’s most prestigious university, announced that due to abnormally high marks in entrance exam results, which raised suspicions of widespread cheating, approximately 58,000 students would have to retake the exam.
Schools worldwide are rushing to deal with AI use by students, either through bans or teaching AI literacy and ethics to pupils. Denmark announced this month that from the new term, teenagers would have to make a verbal defence of their written essays to combat AI cheating.
Eaton has implemented similar reforms in her class. “AI can generate a perfect essay in seconds. So an essay is no longer a viable way to demonstrate students’ learning,” she said. “We should let it die with dignity and find other ways to assess student learning.”
In Eaton’s view, cheating is a “human problem” and so grading and assessing student learning has historically also been a human responsibility, which she said cannot easily be off-loaded to the latest tech.
But what institutions around the world have been doing instead, she said, is hoping technology can be easily and cheaply used to prevent cheating en masse.
Dr Thomas Lancaster, a computer scientist at Imperial College London who researches the educational use of generative AI, has studied how cheating has gone hi-tech. It is no longer about sneaking notes written on a sleeve, he said, but sophisticated communication.
Lancaster says students have always cheated but it has become far easier, with pupils using earpieces or miniature cameras, and increasingly, communicating with friends or family with access to internet searches.
“I think the number of notes you sneak in, they’re only useful if they’re the right notes and if you can interpret them very quickly,” he said.
“But with any kind of communication technology, you can have that expertise waiting outside for you in a human form. They either know the answers; they’ve got the expertise; or they can look them up online. You can go further than that now and have access to AI to communicate directly during the exam as well,” he added. “It’s spy technology, really.”
Rather than individual student misconduct, Eaton said the failure to properly train educators was forcing students to spend valuable time trying to navigate through these “broken” systems rather than focusing on learning.
“This idea of these mass examinations happening at scale, it sort of dehumanises the learning process and the assessment process,” she said.
This, she said, “sort of perpetuates this idea that examinations are the most important thing for students. I think we’ve lost the thread. Learning should be the most important thing for students.”
