The 1991 treaty that still haunts the Kremlin
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

As Russia wages war on Ukraine, denying its own history of aggression against its neighbours, a little-known treaty signed 35 years ago is quietly getting in the way of the Kremlin's narrative.
In July 1991, five months before the Soviet Union formally dissolved, Russia signed a treaty with Lithuania in which it recognised something Moscow has spent the past two decades trying to erase from the record: that the Soviet Union illegally annexed Lithuania in 1940, and that Lithuania's independence, declared in 1990, was legitimate.
That document is still in force. Russia has never torn it up. And that, say Lithuanian officials and legal experts, makes it an awkward loose end for a Kremlin that now insists the Baltic states were never really occupied at all.
Under President Vladimir Putin, Russian state doctrine has increasingly leaned on the claim that the Baltic states joined the USSR voluntarily, and that talk of "occupation" is Western-backed revisionism.
That framing underpins not only Moscow's rhetoric towards Lithuania, Latvia and Estonia but also, in a broader sense, the ideological justification for its invasion of Ukraine – the idea that the borders and independence of post-Soviet states are negotiable, or fictional.
The 1991 treaty directly contradicts that. Dainius Žalimas, a Lithuanian MEP who has studied the treaty closely, argues that its first article, which has no expiry date, remains one of Lithuania's strongest legal cards: it commits both countries to recognising each other as sovereign states with an inalienable right to independence, and bars either from meddling in each other's internal affairs as well as using or threatening force against the other.
"All of this essentially refutes every claim made by current official Russian propaganda, and undermines the very foundations of the ideology of 'rashism'. That ideology, which underpins both the aggression against Ukraine and the denial of Baltic statehood, recognises neither the occupation of Lithuania nor the continuity of the Lithuanian state, among much else," said Žalimas.
"I think the treaty is very important. We talk about the wavering authority of international law, but it gives Lithuania a solid line of international legal defence, and shows that Russia, for very brief periods in its history, can be different," he added.
For example, Lithuania can continue to demand compensation from Russia for the damage caused by occupation, even though few believe this will ever happen in practice.
"Just as the treaty of July 12, 1920 formed the basis for all subsequent treaties with the Soviet Union, the same applies here – dozens of Lithuanian-Russian agreements rest on the July 29, 1991 treaty. So the treaty still holds, as long as they haven't broken it," explained Česlovas Vytautas Stankevičius, former deputy chairman of the Supreme Council and head of the negotiating delegation with Russia, highlighting why the treaty still carries weight to this day.
How it came about
The path to the treaty began in secrecy. In May 1990, Vytautas Landsbergis, then chairman of Lithuania's Supreme Council (the country's parliament in the final Soviet years), held a clandestine meeting with Boris Yeltsin, his Russian counterpart, in an empty Moscow school classroom. The two men barely knew each other personally, and the meeting was arranged through informal contacts.
Two months later, leaders of the three Baltic states and Russia met in the Latvian town of Jūrmala and agreed to pursue bilateral treaties – partly as a way of applying pressure on Mikhail Gorbachev, the Soviet leader, who was still trying to hold the union together.
That effort was not merely symbolic. The USSR spent 1990 and 1991 trying to force Lithuania, as well as other republics, back into line, including a 75-day economic blockade after its declaration of independence.
At the time, it was unclear whether it was better to negotiate with the Soviet leadership or with Russia, which in June 1990 had issued its own declaration of state sovereignty. As a result, negotiations proceeded with both the Soviet authorities and with Yeltsin's Russia, which proved to be a great decision since once the Soviet Union collapsed, Russia became its legal successor.
For Lithuania, negotiating and signing the treaty meant securing the backing of the largest Soviet republic for its independence – a powerful argument against the Soviet Union. Meanwhile, Russia's leadership was competing during with the central Soviet authorities for control.
"The goals of the two states coincided," summarized Stankevičius, who was appointed head of Lithuania's negotiating delegation.
Having concluded treaties with Latvia and Estonia, Russia wanted to sign a similar document with Lithuania.
But Lithuania's treaty took longer to conclude, largely because Vilnius pushed for tougher language than Moscow wanted to accept.
The sticking points
Negotiators clashed over three issues in particular.
The first was citizenship. Lithuania wanted to prevent members of the Soviet military and security apparatus – as well as their families – from automatically qualifying for Lithuanian citizenship, while also avoiding a settlement that would let ethnic Russians resident in the country claim dual nationality.
A compromise formula eventually restricted citizenship rights to those with ties to Russia who had lived and worked in Lithuania since before November 1989.
The second was how to characterise the movement of people under Soviet rule – specifically, how to avoid a formula that would have put Lithuanians deported to Russia by Stalin's regime in the same legal category as Russians who had been resettled into Lithuania as part of Soviet colonisation policy.
The two sides eventually agreed on the neutral term "relocating persons" to cover both groups without equating them politically.
Finally, the thorniest question – whether Russia would accept responsibility for the 1940 annexation – was resolved not by the negotiating teams but by Landsbergis and Yeltsin personally.
"The Russian negotiators didn't want to accept responsibility for crimes committed by the Bolsheviks – all those acts of violence and coercion against neighbouring states. They didn't want the responsibility. Their position was that the Russian Federation, in restoring its own statehood, would not take on responsibility for the crimes of the communist regime," Stankevičius told LRT.lt.
Meanwhile, Landsbergis's own public position at the time was that responsibility lay with the USSR rather than Russia specifically.
So, the compromise text kept a reference to the 1940 annexation while dropping Lithuania's request to invoke an earlier, 1920 peace treaty in which Soviet Russia had renounced all claims to Lithuanian territory, making it's later Soviet occupation – illegal.
The irony
That compromise proved more significant than either side may have anticipated. When the Soviet Union collapsed months later, Russia inherited its rights and obligations as successor state – including, Lithuanian officials argue, responsibility for the consequences of the 1940 annexation that Russia itself had just agreed to acknowledge. And so, it was obliged to remedy the damage, withdraw its troops, and so on.
Three and a half decades on, the treaty's core value lies precisely in the fact that it is Moscow's own signature on a document that says the opposite of what the Kremlin now claims.

‘No Room for Mistakes’: What Next for Protestors Demanding Fedorov’s Reinstatement?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:56:00 UTC
15:56:00 KST

On Sunday Aug. 16, a significant rally in support of Mykhailo Fedorov, along with a demand for the continuation of reforms at the Ministry of Defense, is due to take place in central Kyiv. However, the focus of recent protests is already shifting beyond these boundaries, with many of the banners and placards no longer mentioning Fedorov.
                                                                                                                                                                         
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
At the Aug. 14 rally, broader grievances against the authorities came to the fore – corruption, non-transparent decision-making, not listening to the people, and even the cutting down of 100-year-old oak trees in one of Kyiv’s districts for the benefit of developers, which has stirred anger among local residents.
                                                                                                                                                                         
One of the main slogans of last week’s large march, which gathered more than 10,000 people, was “Listening to the people – that’s powerful,” where the word “powerful,” which President Volodymyr Zelensky had previously used extensively, was employed as a form of trolling.
Zelensky Confirms Flamingo Missile Strikes Deep in Russian Territory
However, the future of the rallies is now very much in question.
                                                                                                                                                                         
Many commentators believe that, despite the uncertainty surrounding Fedorov himself (he said that negotiations with representatives of the authorities are still ongoing), President Zelensky is unlikely to be considering the option of returning him to the position of head of the Ministry of Defense.
Why?
First, General Yevhenii Khmara has already been introduced to key players and foreign partners as acting minister of defense; and second, the president cannot risk showing to the hierarchy that he is willing to give in to the street on everything.
                                                                                                                                                                         
The situation is made even more uncertain by Fedorov himself. He has not come out to the people on Maidan – that would have meant his transition into active politics – and he has indicated a lack of desire to criticize President Zelensky until the end of the war.
In a conversation with prominent civic activist Serhiy Sternenko, Fedorov said: “I will not lead the protest, because it will be perceived as a political move. And our task is to win the war.”
According to several journalists and political strategists, this is a mistake.
Ukrainian journalist Maryna Danyliuk-Yermolayeva stated. “Yes, Yushchenko’s emergence among the wider masses as a talented economist turned him into an icon of the national democrats. Yulia Tymoshenko’s appearances among the people cemented her for a long time as the people’s darling, the princess of Ukrainian politics. The same ride on a road grader by Petro Poroshenko at the dawn of the Revolution of Dignity instantly propelled the former minister in Azarov’s government to the top of the ratings… Fedorov said in an interview with The New York Times that he would not be in opposition until the end of the war. But it is worth saying that after that there will be new heroes. And they will certainly know how to go out into the streets and meet with people.”
The future of the protests therefore look ambiguous.
The largest rally, “The Last Attempt to Reach the President,” is due to take place on Sunday Aug. 16. On Tuesday Aug. 18, during parliament’s plenary week, lawmakers will vote for the new head of the Ministry of Defense.
Will the rallies continue if it becomes clear that Fedorov will definitely not return to his former ministerial post?
And if they do continue, how long can they last?
For now, this question remains open. All the more so because the main grievance of part of society toward Zelensky is not so much about Fedorov as it is about the systemic problems that forced Zelensky to dismiss the defense minister – even knowing that this would cause dissatisfaction.

How the US could squeeze Iran with economic isolation – and the risks involved

How the US could squeeze Iran with economic isolation – and the risks involved
The Treasury Department is exploring targeting Chinese banks, shadow fleets and exchange houses, but secondary sanctions risk economic blowback
Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent says the US is getting ready to squeeze Iran with unprecedented economic pressure, a claim critics greeted with scepticism given the country is already subject to a naval blockade and thousands of sanctions.
While the Trump administration has not said what it is planning to do, there are still pressure points that Bessent’s Treasury Department could hit. The main challenge is that targeting the remaining options risks blowback on the US economy.
“Unless the president decides to prioritise addressing the Iran threat over all other issues, and namely China, it’s unlikely any action they take is going to materially change Iran’s calculus,” Bloomberg Economics analyst Chris Kennedy said.
Below is a look at a few options. They are far from exhaustive and it remains unclear which, if any, the administration will pursue. Officials could combine several of these measures or opt for a different approach altogether.
China buys more than 90 per cent of Iran’s oil exports. Penalties on entities that facilitate these purchases would directly reduce Tehran’s oil revenues.
Washington has already sanctioned some Chinese teapot refineries and firms since the US started the war against Iran in late February. But so far, the US has stopped short of targeting the major Chinese banks that finance the trade.
The risk is that hitting Chinese companies or financial institutions risks worsening tensions with Beijing ahead of a planned meeting between US President Donald Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping. There is also an economic trade-off, since curtailing Iranian barrels would remove discounted crude from the global market and could lift already elevated oil prices.

Troops from North Korea to boost Russia's Ukraine forces
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:49:00 UTC
15:49:00 KST

Troops from North Korea to boost Russia's Ukraine forces
August 16, 2026
In late 2024, North Korea sent troops to help defend the Kursk region of Russia from Ukraine. International officials estimate that 14,000-15,000 North Korean troops were initially deployed to assist Russia in its war in Ukraine..
Now, Ukrainian and international intelligence agencies suspect that North Korea is planning to send an additional 30,000-50,000 troops to Russia. Ukrainian officials have also stated that North Korean ballistic missiles are being fired from Russia.
President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has raised concerns that North Korea's improved combat and weapons experience could pose a security threat to Ukraine, as well as the Asia-Pacific region.
Defense in Russia
Observers have had mixed responses to Zelenskyy's remarks. Oleksandr Kovalenko, a military and political expert at the Ukrainian Center for Security Studies, told DW that new troops from North Korea would likely be used to secure Russia's border territories. Aside from remaining in Kursk, North Korean soldiers could also help secure southwestern Vornezh.
"These forces could free up Russian occupational forces," Kovalenko said. "Russia would then be able to deploy around 15,000 troops to battles along front lines in Vovchansk, Kharkiv, Sumy or even Chernihiv."
Kovalenko does not expect North Korean troops to fight on Ukrainian territory.
The Russian military analyst Ruslan Leviev agrees. "It's one thing to recapture Russian territory," he told DW, "and an entirely different thing to be present on internationally recognized Ukrainian territory, taking part in a war of aggression."
"Diplomatically speaking," he said, "those are two entirely different concepts."
Russia not outgunned
Leviev said he had not seen concrete evidence of a noteworthy North Korean presence in Russia. He said Moscow had enough troops available without having to draw foreign soldiers into the fight.
"If you look at the front line, you can see that Russia's forces have been fairly active since mid-July," Leviev said. "That's a clear sign they still have enough forces."
Estimates by the economist Janis Kluge, of the German Institute for International and Security Affairs (SWP), back this up. "While Russia's recruitment campaign has shown signs of strain this year, regional spending figures indicate that Russia is still recruiting around 1,000 contract soldiers each day," he recently wrote in a report.
Intelligence on North Korea's short- and medium-range ballistic systems, the KN-23 and KN-24, is far less speculative. Ukraine's Main Directorate of Intelligence (HUR) states that North Korea has delivered 148 KN-23 and KN-24 missiles since 2025.
In July, Zelenskyy said one such missile had struck a home near Ukraine's Kryvyi Rih, killing two parents and three children.
The HUR recently reported that a North Korean missile unit was deploying to Russia's Voronezh region, where it could be equipped with 120 ballistic missiles. Zelenskyy has taken this as indication that Russia's strategic reserve is depleted.
Kovalenko has come to a different conclusion. Instead of having run out of reserves, he said, he believes that Russia is looking to launch a higher number of missiles "to spread more terror."
'Instruments of terror'
Ukrainian military sources say North Korean missiles are similar to Russia's Iskander missiles, but with a greater payload, longer range and lower accuracy.
The military expert Yuri Fyodorov calls North Korea's missiles "instruments of terror." He told DW that "it is very difficult to hit a small target with North Korean weapons." He said he believed that Russia had deliberately targeted residential areas with these missiles.
Like the Russian Iskander 9M723, KN-23 and KN-24 missiles can only be stopped by US-made Patriot air-defense systems, which Ukraine has run critically low on.
Leviev said ballistic missiles would play a key role in this phase of the war. As Ukraine's stockpiles run low, he said, it will become a growing priority to Russia to keep a steady supply.
This summer, Leviev said, he has seen Russia launch far more ballistic missiles than in previous years. Should Russia's missile supply run low by winter, he said, "there will still be North Korea reserves, to make sure Ukraine cannot catch its breath."
No turning point
None of the experts DW spoke with believes that North Korea's involvement will bring about a turning point in Russia's war with Ukraine.
Jenny Town, of the US-based Foreign Policy Research Institute, said the time had passed for North Korea's involvement to make a significant difference. Instead, she said, Russia and North Korea have established a mutually beneficial partnership that is only likely to deepen through the course of the war with Ukraine.
Kovalenko also doesn't expect Russia to make substantial territorial gains along the front lines, but he is concerned about the upcoming winter. "October, November and December could become very trying for Ukraine," he said.
Russian forces could benefit from a possible new military call up, from freeing up fighters it replaces with North Korean troops and from a further stream of ballistic missiles. "That would be no good for the course of the war, and for Ukraine's war interests," Fyodorov said.
This article was originally written in Russian.

General Staff: Russia has lost 1,467,320 troops in Ukraine since Feb. 24, 2022
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:45:57 UTC
15:45:57 KST

Russia has lost around 1,467,320 troops in Ukraine since the beginning of its full-scale invasion on Feb. 24, 2022, the General Staff of the Armed Forces of Ukraine reported on Aug. 16.
The figure includes 1,510 casualties Russian forces suffered over the past day.
According to the report, Russia has also lost 12,270 tanks, 25,151 armored combat vehicles, 134,851 vehicles and fuel tanks, 47,007 artillery systems, 2,034 multiple launch rocket systems, 1,570 air defense systems, 439 aircraft, 354 helicopters, 463,404 unmanned aerial vehicles, 2,257 unmanned ground systems, 35 ships and boats, and two submarines.
Ukraine's General Staff has not revealed its own losses during the full-scale invasion, citing operational secrecy.
Independent Western think-tank reports agree that the Russian casualties significantly surpass Ukraine's losses, with the Center for Strategic and International Studies (CSIS) estimating the ratio to be "roughly 2.5:1 or 2:1."
A January 2026 CSIS report said Ukraine has likely suffered between 500,000 and 600,000 casualties from February 2022 to December 2025, of which between 100,000 to 140,000 are thought to be killed in action (KIA).
While Ukrainian officials rarely disclose the figure, President Volodymyr Zelensky told France TV on Feb. 4 that at least 55,000 Ukrainian soldiers have been killed in action since the beginning of the full-scale war, in addition to a lot more missing in action (MIAs). The intensity of Russian drones and the fighting has made it extremely difficult for Ukraine to retrieve the bodies of fallen soldiers, which are needed for DNA confirmation.

War update: 212 clashes on front lines over past day, Pokrovsk sector hottest
According to Ukrinform, the General Staff of the Ukrainian Armed Forces reported this in a Facebook update on the situation as of 08:00 on Sunday, August 16.
Russian forces launched 99 airstrikes, dropping 312 guided aerial bombs. They also used 11,235 kamikaze drones and carried out 3,122 shelling attacks, including 64 using multiple-launch rocket systems.
Artillery fire hit Koreniok, Sopych, Bachivsk, Neskuchne, Bezsalivka, Rohizne, Ryzhivka, Hirky, Ulanove and Volfyne in the Sumy region, as well as Khrinivka and Mykolaivka in the Chernihiv region. Koreniok and Ulanove came under airstrikes.
Ukrainian aircraft, missile forces and artillery struck 12 areas where Russian personnel were concentrated, seven artillery systems and one UAV command post.
In the Northern Slobozhanshchyna and Kursk sectors, Ukrainian forces repelled four Russian assaults. Russian troops carried out 49 attacks on Ukrainian positions and settlements.
In the Southern Slobozhanshchyna sector, Ukrainian units repelled five Russian attacks. Russian invaders attempted to advance near Vovchanski Khutory and toward the settlements of Zybine, Aniskine and Okhrimivka.
In the Kupiansk sector, Russian forces launched ten offensive actions toward Radkivka and Kurylivka.
In the Lyman sector, Russian troops attacked nine times toward Cherneshchyna, Novyi Myr, Drobysheve, Lyman and Dibrova, as well as near Ridkodub and Novoselivka.
In the Sloviansk sector, the enemy launched 21 assaults near Riznykivka, Kryva Luka and Zakitne, and toward Rai-Oleksandrivka and Pyskunivka.
Russian forces conducted no offensive operations in the Kramatorsk sector.
In the Kostiantynivka sector, 28 attacks were recorded. Russian invaders conducted assaults near Kostiantynivka, Ivanopillia, Illinivka, Novoselivka, Sofiivka and Rusyn Yar, and toward Vilne, Stepanivka, Novopavlivka and Kucheriv Yar.
In the Pokrovsk sector, Russian forces launched 34 attacks. They attempted to advance near Vasylivka, Udačne, Kotlyne and Novopidhorodnie, and toward Bilytske, Novyi Donbas, Shevchenko, Novohryshyne, Serhiivka and Novopavlivka.
In the Oleksandrivka sector, Russian invaders launched two attacks toward Oleksandrohrad and Rybne.
In the Hulyaipole sector, Russian troops attacked Ukrainian positions six times, attempting to advance toward Charivne, Novoselivka and Tsvitkove, as well as near Bilohiria.
In the Orikhiv sector, Ukrainian forces stopped three Russian attempts to advance toward Stepnohirsk and Plavni.
Russian forces conducted no offensive operations on the Dnipro River axis.
No signs of Russian offensive force buildup were detected on the Volyn and Polissia axes.

Taichung uses AI cameras, drones to crack down on illegal open burning
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:43:58 UTC
15:43:58 KST

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — Taichung’s Environmental Protection Bureau said Saturday that its artificial intelligence camera system and aerial drones have detected hundreds of cases of illegal open burning, resulting in NT$1.28 million (US$39,000) in fines, according to a Taichung City Government press release.
The bureau said it began deploying AI smoke-detecting cameras along with drones in 2022. When the camera system detects abnormal air pollution, it alerts authorities, who then deploy drones and send inspectors to the location.
The bureau said the ground-and-air surveillance network has caught hundreds of cases of unauthorized open burning, with fines totaling NT$1.28 million. The bureau added that nearly 170 AI smoke-detection systems have been installed across Taichung to improve enforcement efficiency.
The bureau said open burning harms air quality and may cause fires. Under the Air Pollution Control Act, violators can be fined up to NT$100,000, while industrial or commercial facilities can face fines of up to NT$5 million, the bureau said.

Chinese ship sets off for Europe through Arctic, halving travel time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:37:00 UTC
15:37:00 KST

Chinese ship sets off for Europe through Arctic, halving travel time
- A Chinese container ship, Dubai Tower, has begun a new route through the Arctic's Northern Sea Route, cutting travel time to Europe by half.
- The route avoids the Suez Canal and Red Sea, reducing risks from Middle East conflicts but is only open during ice melt seasons.
- Environmental groups warn this increased Arctic shipping may speed ice melt, while China promotes the route as part of its "Polar Silk Road" plan.
AI generated
BEIJING – A Chinese container vessel has set sail for Europe through the Arctic, part of new weekly service on a route that cuts shipment times in half but which environmental groups warn could speed up the melting of polar ice.
The Dubai Tower left the eastern port city of Ningbo on the evening of Aug 15 heading north, where it will pass through the Bering Strait, then turn west through the frigid waters along Russia’s northern coast.
It is expected to reach Felixstowe in the United Kingdom on Sept 7, followed by stops in Hamburg, Germany and Gdynia, Poland later that week, according to a timetable published by Sea Legend.
The Northern Sea Route (NSR) from China to Europe is significantly faster than that through the Suez Canal and allows vessels to avoid the Red Sea, where shipping has been targeted by Iran-backed Houthis in Yemen.
But the route is only accessible during the time of the year when the ice is melted enough to allow transits without icebreakers.
Global shipping is heavily disrupted by war in the Middle East, sparked by US-Israeli strikes on Iran in late February.
Other shipping companies have transited the Arctic passage before, with Denmark’s Maersk the first to do so in 2018.
In 2025, 23 container ships transited through, up from 15 in 2024, according to shipping analysis from business insurer Allianz Commercial.
Environmental groups warn that increasing numbers of ships transiting the shorter NSR could accelerate loss of Arctic sea ice – already made vulnerable by rising global temperatures.
Major shipping companies including France’s CMA CGM, Switzerland-headquartered MSC and Germany-based Hapag-Lloyd have pledged to “avoid Arctic trans-shipment routes”.
But analysts say the Arctic could serve as an important future trade route, particularly for China, which has outlined plans for greater access to the region through a “Polar Silk Road”. AFP

Jharkhand students and aspirants protest
NEW DELHI: The protest by students and job aspirants over alleged irregularities in Jharkhand recruitment examinations entered its 23rd day on Sunday, with the agitators announcing plans to burn effigies of chief minister Hemant Soren and congress leader Rahul Gandhi across all 24 districts.The protesters, under the banner of JPSC-JSSC Reforms Manch, accused the ruling JMM-Congress alliance of failing to take “concrete action” on their demands. They also announced plans to gherao Soren's residence in Ranchi on August 20 and demanded his resignation.JPSC-JSSC Reforms Manch leader Ravindra Paswan said the protesters would intensify their agitation unless their demands were addressed.“We will gherao the chief minister's residence on August 20. We seek his resignation as both the JMM and the Congress are playing politics with us. We also demand that Rahul Gandhi withdraw support from the JMM-led coalition if the CM is not listening to him,” Paswan said.The protesters are seeking cancellation of the JSSC-CGL examination and certain Jharkhand Public Service Commission examinations over alleged irregularities. They have also demanded a CBI probe or an inquiry by a panel of retired judges from outside Jharkhand.
Students and job aspirants held a 'Tiranga Yatra' in Ranchi on Saturday to mark India's 80th Independence Day and press for recruitment reforms. Thousands participated in the march from Jaipal Singh Munda Stadium to Albert Ekka Chowk, including two wheelchair-borne protesters who were on hunger strike.Meanwhile, JLKM leader and student activist Devendra Mahto, who has been fasting for 15 days, alleged that police prevented him from joining the march after he tried to leave Sadar Hospital.Videos purportedly showing Mahto in a confrontation with security personnel were circulated on social media. The protesters alleged police high-handedness during the Independence Day programme.This comes as Jharkhand leader of opposition Babulal Marandi urged the state government to engage with the protesting aspirants.
“The government should not remain adamant on its stance. It should pay attention to the students' demands... The government should hold talks with the students without any delay,” Marandi told ANI.However, the state government has said it is taking steps to strengthen the recruitment process. In his Independence Day address, Soren said questions over examination irregularities had shaken the trust of a generation of job aspirants.He said investigations would be impartial and that those found guilty would face strict legal action, while cautioning against declaring anyone guilty without evidence.The state government has proposed an annual examination calendar, technology-secured examinations, timely publication of answer keys and OMR sheets, and multi-level accountability.

South Korea's military last week fired warning shots after North Korean soldiers violated the military demarcation line, prompting them to return, Yonhap news agency reported on Sunday, citing an unnamed source.
Yonhap said multiple North Korean soldiers crossed the military demarcation line (MDL) in the eastern area of the demilitarised zone (DMZ), in the first reported MDL violation by the North Korean military this year.
The soldiers were likely patrolling the area when they crossed the MDL, and no other unusual activity was reported after the North Korean soldiers returned, Yonhap said.
The North Korean embassy in Singapore did not immediately respond to a request for comment.
North Korean troops violated the MDL 17 times last year, the news agency said.
The two Koreas are still technically at war after their 1950-53 war ended in an armistice rather than a peace treaty.
It comes as North Korea denounced upcoming joint military drills by tens of thousands of US and South Korean forces, warning it could respond with stronger deterrence measures.
The 11-day exercises due to begin on Monday are a regular fixture of the summer calendar, designed to prepare the United States and South Korea to defend against the nuclear-armed North.

Across China: Return of delicate aquatic plant signals Chinese lake's revival
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
14:35:11 CST
15:35:11 KST

KUNMING, Aug. 16 (Xinhua) -- Under the summer sun, Erhai Lake in southwest China's Yunnan Province glistens thanks to its clear blue water dotted with floating white blossoms. Tourists stroll and cycle around the spot, where limpid waters, lush mountains and blooming flowers combine in weaving a picturesque scene.
The delicate blossom seen floating there is Ottelia acuminata, an aquatic plant endemic to China, which is known to have extremely strict water quality requirements. It demands high water transparency, cleanliness and calcium content, and survives only in unpolluted waters, serving as a reliable natural indicator of aquatic ecological health.
Its large-scale revival vividly testifies to Erhai Lake's remarkable ecological restoration achievements.
China's Ecological and Environmental Code, enacted on Saturday, stipulates nationwide protection and restoration of river and lake ecosystems, providing solid legal support for systematic water ecological governance across the country.
As China advances its Beautiful China initiative, this code is expected to strengthen rule-of-law guarantees for ecological protection and provide fresh impetus for the country's green and low-carbon transition.
In the early 1990s, agricultural pollution worsened Erhai Lake's water quality, causing frequent blue-green algae outbreaks. A severe algae bloom hit the lake in 1996, leaving the water turbid and smelly. As the environment degraded, Ottelia acuminata nearly disappeared from the lake.
Thanks to years of targeted ecological protection and restoration efforts, the lake's water quality has improved steadily, maintaining an excellent Grade II standard from January to June 2026, and creating favorable conditions for the return of Ottelia acuminata.
Official data show that the plant's coverage on Erhai Lake has surged from 90,000 square meters in 2021 to 950,000 square meters in 2026, marking a remarkable ecological recovery.
The improved water environment has also brought tangible economic benefits, turning the lake's ecological advantages into a sustainable industry. From lake ecology to farm cultivation and consumer products, Ottelia acuminata's dual ecological and economic value has been fully realized.
In 2018, environmental worker Zhong Lan noticed the edible plant's great potential for water purification and industrial development. With support from local authorities and Shanghai Jiao Tong University in eastern China, she started a company and built a professional research base in 2024, developing standardized planting techniques that enable high-yield, repeated harvests and offering jobs for local villagers.
Villager Li Xinyuan, who quit Ottelia acuminata cultivation years ago due to low profits, resumed planting in 2023 and earns a stable monthly salary of 4,500 yuan (about 663 U.S. dollars) at the base.
His fellow villager Du Hongquan earned over 100,000 yuan last year from growing the plant and has more than doubled his planting area this year. "The base takes full charge of sales, so we can plant without any worries," Du said.
Eryuan County, the source of Erhai Lake, now has more than 200 hectares of Ottelia acuminata plantation area, creating numerous jobs yearly. The plant has been developed into a diverse range of products like tea, plant-based coffee and cookies.
"I used to think it was exclusive to Yunnan, but I can try it elsewhere too," said a tourist surnamed Ai after tasting the plant at a hotpot restaurant in Hangzhou, east China's Zhejiang Province.
The beautiful lakeside scenery at Erhai has also boosted local tourism. In 2025, Dali received over 120 million tourist visits, attracting visitors to enjoy lake views and experience local Bai ethnic culture, with 407,000 long-term visitors.
Last month, the State Council issued a plan for building a Beautiful China during the 15th Five-Year Plan period (2026-2030), setting out the overall requirements, targets, key tasks and major projects for comprehensively advancing this initiative.
According to the plan, China will achieve overall improvement in ecological and environmental quality and make significant progress toward building a Beautiful China by 2030, with green production and lifestyles largely established and the carbon peaking target reached on schedule. ■

BEIJING, Aug. 16 (Xinhua) -- China's Ministry of Emergency Management has sent 13 emergency drainage teams to the city of Zhoukou in central China's Henan Province to assist with urban waterlogging relief efforts, the ministry said on Sunday.
The teams from Henan, Shaanxi, Jiangsu, Anhui, Shandong and Hunan provinces, arrived in Zhoukou and began operations by 9 a.m. on Sunday.
They have a combined drainage capacity of about 220,000 cubic meters per hour, according to the ministry.
Henan has been lashed by torrential downpours brought by the remnants of Typhoon Dolphin, the year's 13th typhoon, that landed in east China last Sunday.
Following the heavy rain, a levee along the Jialu River, a tributary of the Shaying River, breached in Zhoukou on Thursday, widening to roughly 117 meters at its peak. The breach was sealed on Saturday night.
The flood peak on the Shaying River has passed through Zhoukou, while water levels in the Zhoukou section are expected to remain above the warning level through Sunday, the Ministry of Water Resources noted. ■

Taiwan groups warn of Taiwan Strait security risks due to Japan's neo-militarism
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
14:34:52 CST
15:34:52 KST

TAIPEI, Aug. 16 (Xinhua) -- Several Taiwan-based groups have voiced opposition to Japan's neo-militarism, warning that Tokyo's push for a greater military role in regional affairs could undermine peace and stability across the Taiwan Strait and throughout East Asia.
Their concerns were expressed at forums, seminars and a public gathering held on Friday and Saturday, as Aug. 15 this year marks the 81st anniversary of Japan's unconditional surrender in World War II.
At a forum in Taipei, more than 10 organizations, including the Reunification Alliance Party, brought together scholars and experts from both sides of the Taiwan Strait, as well as from Hong Kong Special Administrative Region, Japan and Australia.
Participants called on people on both sides of the Strait and across Asia to draw lessons from history, oppose the expansionist ambitions of Japanese right-wing forces, and safeguard peace and stability in East Asia.
Citing remarks previously made by Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi, Wang Yung, president of the Reunification Alliance Party and host of the forum, said they suggested the possibility of Japan's military intervention in Taiwan-related affairs and represented a key sign of the country's accelerating remilitarization.
The Japanese government was seeking to loosen the constraints imposed by its pacifist Constitution and the long-standing post-war principle of an "exclusively defense-oriented policy," Wang noted, warning that such moves could have serious repercussions for security in the Taiwan Strait and peace in East Asia.
A seminar was also held on Saturday in Taichung, central Taiwan, drawing over 30 descendants of resistance fighters who fought against Japanese aggression, as well as historians, teachers and students.
The event was jointly organized by several Taiwan-based groups, including an association representing relatives of patriots who fought against Japanese aggression.
Li Su-hui, daughter of anti-Japanese resistance fighter Li Tsang-chiang, said her family retained painful memories of resistance to Japanese colonial rule in Taiwan from 1895 to 1945.
"Remembering history is not merely about paying tribute to those who came before us, but also about passing on the spirit of resistance from generation to generation," Li said.
She voiced concern over the resurgence of Japanese right-wing forces and signs of militarism re-emerging, saying the seminar could help young people develop a proper understanding of their national identity and history.
"We are part of the Chinese nation, and the lessons of history must not be forgotten," Li said.
Shaw Kai-ping, also a descendant of an anti-Japanese resistance fighter, warned of the risks posed by both a revival of Japanese militarism and growing secessionist activities in Taiwan.
"At a time when the Democratic Progressive Party authorities are vigorously promoting a secessionist narrative, we need to stand up and help more people in Taiwan recognize that our motherland is China and our ancestral homeland is on the Chinese mainland," he said.
On Friday, several civic groups, such as the Reunification Alliance Party and the Labor Party, gathered and protested outside the Japan-Taiwan Exchange Association in Taipei.
Wu Jung-yuan, chairman of the Labor Party, said Japan's war of aggression against China had caused more than 35 million Chinese casualties, while Taiwan endured 50 years of Japanese colonial rule, inflicting profound suffering on people on both sides of the Taiwan Strait.
"Taiwan is part of China," Wu said, calling on people on both sides of the Strait to work together to oppose external interference, safeguard peace and stability across the Strait, and strive for national reunification. ■

Interview: Cooperation with China opens new opportunities for S. Africa's agricultural growth, says industry representative
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
14:34:51 CST
15:34:51 KST

JOHANNESBURG, Aug. 16 (Xinhua) -- Growing agricultural cooperation with China is opening up new opportunities for South Africa's farming sector, giving fresh impetus to production and rural economic development, an industry representative has said.
In an interview with Xinhua, Andre van der Vyver, executive director of the South African Cereals and Oilseeds Trade Association, called enhancing cooperation with China "very important."
"It is a reliable market that we can depend on over the medium to long term," he said.
Under the cooperation, exports would encourage production and add more value to the local economy, specifically the regional and rural economies, he said.
China, a major soybean importer, offers South African producers access to a large and diversified market, van der Vyver said, adding that such access would provide the scale and certainty needed to support the continued expansion of South Africa's agricultural sector.
Applauding China's zero-tariff treatment for qualifying South African products, he said that, combined with South Africa's geographical freight advantage over major competitors, the removal of the tariff provides an important boost to exporters.
The tariff arrangement could also create opportunities for a wider range of agricultural products, including maize and fruit, van der Vyver added.
Beyond commodities, he said, there was considerable scope to deepen cooperation across the agricultural value chain, including agricultural equipment, technology, logistics, infrastructure and market access.
Van der Vyver said that South Africa has already imported Chinese agricultural equipment, and that Chinese expertise and investment in infrastructure could help address major constraints facing the country's agricultural exporters, particularly in rail, roads and ports.
"The interaction of exporting products while leveraging China's technology, equipment, and infrastructure capabilities creates a very valuable bilateral trade relationship moving forward," he said.
"Partnering with China will definitely add strong value to our agricultural sector and rural economies, so we are very excited about it," said van der Vyver. ■

Remains of late Chinese leader Zhu Rongji to be cremated
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
14:34:33 CST
15:34:33 KST

BEIJING, Aug. 16 (Xinhua) -- The remains of late Chinese leader Zhu Rongji will be cremated in Beijing on Tuesday.
Zhu, who passed away on Aug. 12 at the age of 98, had served as a member of the Standing Committee of the Political Bureau of the 14th and 15th Central Committees of the Communist Party of China (CPC), and premier of the State Council.
To mourn his death, national flags will fly at half-mast on Tuesday at Tian'anmen Square, Xinhuamen, the Great Hall of the People, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, seats of provincial-level committees of the CPC and provincial-level governments, Hong Kong Special Administrative Region, Macao Special Administrative Region, border ports, seaports and airports, and Chinese embassies and consulates.
Zhu was praised in an obituary jointly issued by the CPC Central Committee, the Standing Committee of the National People's Congress, the State Council and the National Committee of the Chinese People's Political Consultative Conference, as an excellent Party member, a time-tested and loyal communist fighter, and an outstanding proletarian revolutionist, statesman and leader of the Party and the state. ■

====================================================================================================

'Not where the problems are': Working holiday visa slowdown divides parties
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:31:04 UTC
15:31:04 KST

In Brief:
- The government is slowing down processing of working holiday visa applications.
- The Nationals and One Nation say backpackers aren't the problem, instead placing blame on international student numbers.
The federal government has confirmed it will slow the processing of working holiday visa applications as pressure mounts to reduce net migration numbers.
Nine newspapers first reported on Saturday that the federal government had paused applications for working holiday visas from 24 countries.
But Home Affairs Minister Tony Burke confirmed on Sunday that working holiday visas were still being processed, but they were being handled "more slowly than they were previously".
He did not provide further information about the reason for the slowdown.
However, when asked about the impact of fewer backpackers on the agriculture sector, he pointed to a Pacific workers' scheme as the primary visa for temporary staff.
News that makes sense
Your trusted source for staying up-to-date with the world around you. Get free daily news updates and analysis, straight to your inbox.
"One of the things that happened when we started to improve worker protections on (the Pacific scheme) was ... some of the worst operators said, "if we've got to have better standards for (those) workers, we'll just switch to backpackers".
"That's not the way this system's meant to work."
SBS has contacted the Home Affairs department for comment.
Working holiday visas — often called "backpacker visas" — allow travellers to extend their stay in Australia by undertaking work that addresses labour shortages in Australia — often fruit picking, farm work, mining, or tourism work in regional and remote areas.
Migration in the spotlight again
The number of working holiday visa holders in Australia passed 210,000 in June 2025, outpacing pre-COVID figures by around 100,000.
The move comes as pressure builds on the Albanese government to bring down net overseas migration, with other parties carving out their migration policy.
Migration experts have warned of the dangers of political parties attempting to blame migrants for a lack of homes, arguing that migrant workforces will be essential to building the required housing stock
Net overseas migration — a measure covering visitors, family and partner visas, working holidaymakers, skilled migration, and humanitarian intake, alongside returning Australians — fell slightly to 301,000 in 2025, down from 306,000 the year before, according to the Australian Bureau of Statistics.
Labor's long-term target is 225,000 a year over the next three years, though Burke said on Sunday the party's migration policy was still being worked through and needed to go through a full cabinet process.
None of the major parties has yet specified which visa categories they'd cut to hit their targets — though the Coalition has said intake should be tied to housing competitions rather than a fixed annual figure.
Coalition frontbencher Andrew Bragg raised eyebrows last week when he floated cutting net overseas migration to 180,000 a year, suggesting the figure could be reached through "modest reductions" to international students, working holidaymakers and post-study work visas — a mix he said would bring housing supply back in line with population growth.
The Coalition itself is yet to land on an annual migration target or a formal policy for reducing the intake.
Nationals reject changes for backpackers
But the Nationals and One Nation have historically backed working holiday visas for the role they play supporting regional and remote areas, and both parties' leaders spoke out against the government's move on Sunday.
"The system we've got with working holiday visas has worked — and worked for a long time," Nationals leader Matt Canavan said on Sunday.
"It's not where the problems are."
Canavan said there was still a problem with "too many" people coming into Australia and settling in metropolitan areas, which he said was driving "massive housing stress" and pressures on infrastructure — but said backpackers weren't part of that.
"It's not backpackers that are taking away houses from people in Sydney and Melbourne or Brisbane," Canavan said, instead pointing to what he called an "unprecedented surge in student visas" as the real problem, backing a cap on international student numbers at 25 per cent of the current intake.
"We don't think our universities should be skewed and biased towards trying to find an income stream to teach overseas students — they should be skewed and biased towards teaching Australians and doing Australian research," he said.
"That's the system that has to change and where we should put our focus on."
One Nation leader Pauline Hanson, who wants net migration cut to 130,000 a year, agreed the reduction should come from international students, calling the university intake system a "scam" that she claimed provided a backdoor to Australia.
But she was firm that any cuts should leave backpacker numbers alone.
"We cannot stop those people coming in; if they did, the farmers would go under," Hanson said on Sunday.
'One Nation has gone too far': Nationals
On Saturday, Hanson also called for the government to immediately block migration — including refugee and humanitarian visas — from countries on the Department of Foreign Affairs' "do not travel" list, arguing they pose a security risk.
Canavan distanced himself from that proposal on Sunday, saying he was "very concerned" by it.
"One Nation has just gone too far," he said.
"The very reason that many of these countries are on the 'do not travel' list is because they are host to some of the most tyrannical and oppressive regimes in the world … it is in these parts of the world where most of the persecuted Christians and other minorities live."
"I think the question to One Nation here is: if you go this far, aren't we just losing something of what it means to be Australian, that we are welcoming people … and we do welcome people who want to be in this country that contribute to it and ultimately become fantastic Australians?"
Labor MP Chris Bowen dismissed Hanson's idea on Saturday and compared it to something United States President Donald Trump would enact.
"Australia needs an Australian immigration policy, not one stolen from Donald Trump," he said.
"Some of the countries on that list have provided Australia with wonderful migrants ... Iraq, Lebanon, countries like Afghanistan.
"People who have fled ISIS, who have come to Australia for refuge and knowing they've received refuge, have started small businesses, contributed to our community".
— With additional reporting by Australian Associated Press.

Ukrainian athlete Yaroslava Mahuchikh won the gold medal in the women’s high jump at the 2026 European Athletics Championships in Birmingham, England, clearing a height of 1.97 meters, Ukrinform reported.
Mahuchikh entered the competition at 1.92 meters, clearing the bar on her first attempt. She required a second attempt to clear 1.95 meters.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
Fellow Ukrainian Iryna Herashchenko, who had cleared 1.83 meters, 1.88 meters, and 1.92 meters, failed to clear 1.95 meters and finished in fifth place.
With the bar raised to 1.97 meters, Mahuchikh cleared the height on her first attempt, securing the gold medal. She subsequently made three unsuccessful attempts at 2.01 meters.
Poland’s Maria Zhodzik secured the silver medal, and Montenegro’s Marija Vukovic took bronze. Both athletes cleared 1.95 meters but failed all three attempts at 1.97 meters.
The victory in Birmingham marks Mahuchikh’s third consecutive European Championship title, following her wins in 2022 and 2024.
According to Suspilne Sport, Mahuchikh tied the record for the most European Championship titles in the high jump.
With this victory, Mahuchikh tied the record set by Spain’s Ruth Beitia, who had previously been the only athlete to become a three-time European champion in the high jump. She won three consecutive gold medals in 2012, 2014, and 2016.
Beitia also became an Olympic champion in 2016, but she never won a world championship. Mahuchikh won gold at the 2023 World Championships.

Mahuchikh’s European title follows her gold medal victory at the World Indoor Championships in Toruń, Poland, in March, where she cleared 2.01 meters. Ukrainian athlete Yuliia Levchenko secured silver at the same event with a jump of 1.99 meters.
These athletic achievements occur against the backdrop of ongoing debates regarding the participation of athletes from Russia and Belarus in international sports.
In February, the International Paralympic Committee allowed a limited number of Russian and Belarusian athletes to compete in the 2026 Games under their national flags.
Separately, the International Olympic Committee disqualified Ukrainian skeleton racer Vladyslav Heraskevych from the 2026 Winter Olympics after he refused to remove a helmet depicting Ukrainian athletes killed during the war.

The European Union takes note of this commitment and welcomes it
Additionally
The sanctions against Iran were introduced in 2023 over its support for Russia’s aggression against Ukraine, as well as its support for armed groups and structures in the Middle East and the Red Sea region.
The decision to extend the sanctions against Iran was adopted on July 24, 2026.

Twelve killed and at least 10 injured in Polish bus crash in Hungary
BUDAPEST, Aug 16 - A dozen people were killed and at least 10 injured after a Polish bus overturned at night on a motorway in eastern Hungary, Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar said on Sunday morning in a Facebook post.
The bus ran into a ditch and tipped over early on Sunday near the town of Mezokeresztes in eastern Hungary, police said, adding that the bus was heading towards the city of Nyiregyhaza on the motorway when the accident happened.
State news agency MTI said there were 57 passengers and two drivers on the bus. Pictures from the scene showed a badly damaged red bus lying on one side in a ditch by the motorway with windows smashed and belongings and suitcases scattered around.

Twelve people lost their lives and several others were injured in the accident, police said, adding that they were taking the bus driver into custody.
The injured have been taken to hospital.
Polish Foreign Minister Radoslaw Sikorski said on X that a "tragic accident" involving a bus carrying Polish tourists happened in Hungary.
"The consular service is operational. We are in contact with the Hungarian authorities. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs will provide verified information," he said. REUTERS

Thousands of evacuees await aid after deadly quake on Indonesia's Flores island
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:25:43 UTC
15:25:43 KST

Thousands of evacuees await aid after deadly quake on Indonesia's Flores island
Thousands of evacuees on Indonesia's Flores island began receiving aid on Sunday after a 7.7-magnitude quake killed 48 people, injured dozens and damaged hundreds of houses and buildings. Some 5,000 people fled their homes and in some areas people were left without electricity and telecommunications.
Help started arriving Sunday for thousands of evacuees on Indonesia's Flores island after a powerful earthquake killed 48 people, injured dozens and damaged hundreds of houses and buildings.
Some 5,000 people fled their homes as the 7.7-magnitude quake hit the island early Saturday, followed by at least 341 aftershocks in the hours that followed, a spokesman for the BNPB national disaster agency, Berton Suar Pelita Panjaitan, said.
People in some areas were left without electricity and telecommunications and at least one emergency access road remained inaccessible.
"We need food, drink, medicines, and diapers for the children," Umri, a resident of Nangahale village on the north coast of Flores, told AFP on Sunday morning.
Read moreIndonesia hit by second powerful earthquake in one day as death toll rises to 47
The 55-year-old, who like many Indonesians uses one name, has been hiding out on a hilltop for 24 hours with others afraid to return home.
Nurhidayati, also 55, spent the night on a mat on the same hill.
"I'm still terrified; my heart is pounding hard remembering yesterday's tremor ... We just ran up here yesterday. I'm very scared now, still traumatised by the quake in 1992. I don't dare to go home," she said.
Flores was struck by a 7.7-magnitude earthquake in 1992 that triggered a tsunami and killed about 2,500 people.
"I hope the government will soon distribute logistics to help us," said Nurhidayati.
BNPB head Suharyanto said Sunday at least five helicopters, several planes, a naval vessel and search and rescue ships were were on their way to help.
Emergency response
Berton said at least 101 Flores residents were reported injured in the quake.
Survivors were in urgent need of assistance in the form of emergency tents, food, medicines, blankets, camp beds, mats, clean water and electricity generators, he added.
The death toll rose by one from Saturday night to Sunday morning.
Local search and rescue official Fathur Rahman told AFP on Sunday there were no reports of people buried or missing.
The epicentre of the shallow quake was located just off the north coast of the island, geological authorities said.
The affected areas have few, if any, high-rise buildings.
Berton said at least 914 houses were severely damaged and hundreds of others to a lesser degree.
Dozens of public facilities were also stricken, including 93 educational and 36 health facilities, and 38 government offices.
The International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies said Saturday preparations were under way to bring aid to thousands in temporary shelters.
The government sent 50,000 aid packages weighing a total of 275 tonnes to the area and European Union chief Ursula von der Leyen offered help from the bloc's Copernicus Earth observation satellites.
"Europe stands ready to deploy Copernicus, our eyes in the sky, to support search and rescue efforts. Indonesia, we stand with you," she wrote on X.
'Ring of Fire'
In Ruteng, a town about 200 kilometres (124 miles) from the worst-hit part of the island, AFP saw heavy machinery clearing rubble from the street Sunday.
Yohana Raisita Damalia Mari, a spokeswoman for the local hospital, said the building had been badly damaged, and patients were evacuated to tents erected outside.
A woman underwent an emergency C-section in an intact storage room converted into a temporary operating theatre.
"Thank God, the surgery went smoothly and both the patient and her baby are safe," said Yohana.
Herybertus Geradus Laju Nabit, mayor of the Manggarai regency, told AFP it was impossible to predict when the aftershocks would end.
"Therefore, our first appeal is: be vigilant. Stay alert," he said.
After Saturday's quake, Flores residents ran for the hills as the sea retreated, a possible sign of an approaching tsunami.
A tsunami warning was later lifted, but authorities urged people not to return to damaged buildings for fear that aftershocks may bring them down.
Waves of up to 1.6 metres (5.2 feet) were reported in some coastal regions, according to Indonesia's BMKG geological agency.
Indonesia experiences frequent earthquakes due to its location in the Pacific "Ring of Fire", an arc of intense seismic activity stretching from Japan through Southeast Asia and across the Pacific basin.
A devastating 9.1-magnitude tremor in 2004 struck off the coast of Sumatra and triggered a tsunami that killed 220,000 throughout the region, about 170,000 of them in Indonesia.
It was one of the deadliest natural disasters in recorded history.
(FRANCE 24 with AFP)

NATO fighter jet shoots down drone in Romania near border with Ukraine
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:23:43 UTC
15:23:43 KST

NATO fighter jet shoots down drone in Romania near border with Ukraine
Romania's defense ministry said Sunday a NATO fighter jet shot down a drone that had entered its airspace near the border with two of its neighboring countries Ukraine and Moldova.
The ministry said in a statement the drone had entered from Moldova and was intercepted by an Spanish F-18 jet deployed on NATO duties early Sunday morning. Debris fell in an unpopulated area near the border with Ukraine, the ministry added.
The interception is the latest in a series of similar recurring incidents involving drones straying into Ukraine's neighboring countries borders, which authorities have blamed on electronic interference as part of the war with Russia. In some cases, the drones are Ukrainian and are "hijacked" by Russian forces.
Authorities have reported finding drone fragments on Romanian soil several times since 2023 following Russian attacks on Ukrainian ports along the Danube River, which lie just across the border. Last week, Ukraine denied targeting Bulgaria after a drone entered the country's airspace on Saturday and exploded close to an international gas pipeline shortly after crossing the border from Romania.
Last month, Romania shot down a drone for the first time since Russia launched its invasion of Ukraine in 2022, after it entered the country's airspace about 60 miles from Bucharest.
In June, Latvia's military said two NATO fighter jets shot down a drone that entered the country's airspace. Ukrainian drones have previously crashed in all three Baltic states, which all share borders with Russia, with officials blaming the inadvertent incursion on Russian electronic warfare.

====================================================================================================

Avocado pickers in Mexico welcome restart of exports to US, but security fears remain
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:23:02 UTC
15:23:02 KST

Avocado pickers in Mexico welcome restart of exports to US, but security fears remain
SANTA ANA ZIROSTO, Mexico (AP) — It didn’t take long for Mexican avocado picker Francisco Isidro to get back to work after authorities announced the lifting of a U.S. security alert that temporarily halted avocado exports.
Back on the job the morning after the alert was lifted, Isidro threw a rope over an avocado tree about 20 feet (6 meters) high and climbed up. Fifteen minutes later, he had filled a box with avocados bound for the United States.
“Thank God ... and now we’re getting paid!” he shouted happily after several days without work.
Eight days after the alert affecting Michoacán state and the deployment of more Mexican troops in the region, U.S. authorities fully lifted the restrictions that spurred producers to shut down operations, and exports resumed. Michoacán is Mexico’s main avocado-producing state and a region where four cartels designated by the Trump administration as terrorist organizations operate.
By the weekend, orchards were operating again, packing plants were running at full speed and U.S. Department of Agriculture inspectors had returned to certify the fruit and ensure it was free of pests before entering the United States.
The workers were happy to get their daily wages back. Some producers hoped the increased security would reduce violence and extortion. Others feared the calm would not last long.
“We’ll be safe for a while, we’ll see what happens next,” said Valentín Rodríguez, a longtime avocado industry businessperson.
Many threats are possible in a violent state
The U.S. alert caught Isidro high in a tree in an orchard in Santa Ana Zirosto, an area of green, low hills in western Michoacán where criminal groups are very active. There were no explanations, just the foreman’s shout to stop cutting.
Isidro, 39 years old and with two decades of experience as a harvester, knew that this meant either starting to look for another job until the situation returned to normal — since they’re paid by the day — or supporting his family solely on what his wife earned from a small store.
More than 90 miles (145 kilometers) away, in the town of Tacámbaro, an engineer at an avocado packing plant received the alert in the early hours of the morning: The facility should be kept sealed and under quarantine.
Some 200,000 people employed by Michoacán’s avocado industry were left in limbo.
Authorities did not say what threat triggered the alert. But in a state where numerous local cartels make money not only from drugs but also from extortion, there are plenty of possibilities.
Some growers have come to consider extortion an unavoidable production cost. A producer from Michoacán told The Associated Press recently that he pays 1 peso per kilo exported in extortion fees and exports about 90 metric tons a day, which amounts to more than $5,000 in daily payments.
In March alone, Mexico shipped nearly 4,800 tons of avocados a day to the United States.
Trucks loaded with avocados are also sometimes robbed on roads in western Michoacán. And some farmworkers have been stopped and beaten by armed men near the border with Jalisco without being told why, according to one worker who spoke on condition of anonymity for fear of retaliation.
Mexican avocado production is US-controlled
U.S. inspectors have been assaulted and temporarily detained in the past, triggering similar export suspensions. On some occasions, threats arose after inspectors detected pests and were pressured not to report them, said an official familiar with their work who spoke on condition of anonymity for security reasons. The U.S. Embassy does not usually provide details about the incidents.
Inspectors now have less of a presence in the orchards, which are located in isolated hills where armed groups operate with little interference, and concentrate on packing plants.
“If the United States says that it is suspending technical services for security reasons, it’s impossible to export. If it’s for a plant health, it’s the same,” said Rodríguez, who grows, packs and sells avocados. “We are at the mercy of whatever the U.S. market and government decide to do with the industry.”
There is also a political dimension, he said, adding that Mexico didn’t export avocados to the United States for eight decades after a worm was found in an avocado pit in 1914. The U.S. ban was lifted in 1997 as domestic production could no longer meet growing demand.
Exports rely on inspection and certification
More than 80% of Mexican avocados are sold to the U.S. Thousands of tons of avocados travel daily to the United States, especially at the beginning of the year, when demand for guacamole surges ahead of the Super Bowl. To keep that volume moving, certification is key.
Isidro is a “certified” picker. He knows how to disinfect cutting tools before using them, handle the fruit quickly and carefully, and report any spots or damage. The orchards where he works are also certified, providing dining and bathroom facilities for workers.
Jesús Méndez, his supervisor, inspected the boxes before they were loaded onto a truck with the tracking details. The trucks wait until all those in the area are ready before traveling in convoys to packing plants, accompanied by police patrols to prevent robberies.
At the packing plants, inspections continue, checking quality, the fruit’s flesh and possible pests. The avocados then move along mechanical lines that sort them by size before workers place them into boxes.
Once labeled and sealed, the trailers head for the U.S. border. At the slightest security alert, every point along the route can be brought to a standstill.
Fears remain despite the return to work
The deployment of more than 1,500 soldiers to protect Michoacán’s avocado-growing region and recent arrests of people allegedly involved in extortion have eased concerns, but only partially.
Luis Manuel Soto, a 36-year-old grower and packer from western Michoacán, hopes the increased security will bring improvements. So far, he says, he has not felt them.
In 2024, he said, armed men pulled him from his vehicle and threatened to kill him unless he paid them and withdrew a complaint over extortion and an attempt to seize his orchards. The threats returned last July, even though one person involved in the earlier case has been convicted.
“They left me a funeral cross and ... a written message saying I had only days left,” Soto said from a town near Morelia, Michoacán’s capital.
The threats have continued by phone. Now he divides his time between occasional visits to his orchards, managing his businesses and social projects remotely, and going to prosecutors’ offices to request protection.
In Santa Ana Zirosto and surrounding communities, residents welcome the military presence.
“It gives us some peace, but it also scares us a little because it could lead to confrontations with some of the groups,” said Méndez.

COP17 in Mongolia: Why a lesser-known UN summit matters for land, water and food
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-08-16
06:16:23 UTC
15:16:23 KST

Before world leaders gather in Türkiye this November for COP31, the flagship UN climate summit, a lesser-known but increasingly important conference will begin Monday in a country most people associate with steppes, horses and the Gobi Desert.
The 17th Conference of the Parties to the UN Convention to Combat Desertification, or COP17, runs Aug. 17-28 in Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia's capital.
It is the first time a UNCCD COP has been held in East or Central Asia, and it comes to a country where nearly 77% of the land is already degraded.
The Convention to Combat Desertification is one of three "Rio Conventions" that emerged from the 1992 Earth Summit, alongside the climate change and biodiversity treaties. Adopted in 1994, it is the only legally binding international agreement that links environmental protection with land management and rural development, and it now counts 197 parties -- 196 countries plus the European Union.
Its conference of the parties has met roughly every two years since 1997, rotating among regions. COP17 will bring together delegations from nearly 200 countries, along with ministers, scientists, herders, farmers, Indigenous representatives and business leaders for two weeks of negotiations and side events under the theme "Restoring Land, Restoring Hope."
Unlike other climate COPs, which focus on greenhouse gas emissions, global warming and implementation of the Paris Agreement, the UNCCD framework focuses specifically on land degradation, desertification and drought.
This, however, does not mean it is separate from climate change. Drier conditions and rising temperatures can accelerate land degradation, while degraded land can worsen drought impacts, reduce food production and weaken ecosystems.
Desertification does not simply mean deserts expanding. Under the UNCCD definition, it is land degradation in arid, semi-arid and dry sub-humid areas caused by factors including climate variations and human activity. Overgrazing, deforestation, unsustainable farming, poor irrigation, soil erosion and prolonged drought can all contribute.
According to UNCCD, 1.3 billion people are directly exposed to land degradation, while 1.84 billion people endure persistent drought conditions.
It says up to 40% of the world’s land is degraded, undermining food production, water availability and livelihoods, adding that droughts have increased by nearly one-third since 2000.
The pressure is particularly significant for rural populations. Rangelands — grasslands, savannas, shrublands and other areas used extensively for grazing — cover more than half of the Earth's land surface, support about 2 billion people and provide nearly 70% of global livestock feed.
The consequences can extend well beyond the environment. Falling agricultural productivity can threaten food security and incomes, while water shortages and competition for natural resources can contribute to displacement and instability. Migration linked to desertification, land degradation and drought is therefore also on the COP17 agenda.
UNCCD warned that if current trends continue, almost 16 million square kilometers of land — an area roughly the size of South America — could be on a trajectory toward degradation by 2050.
Africa, particularly the Sahel, is among the hardest-hit areas, with millions at risk of displacement as arable land shrinks. African governments have organized as a bloc — the African Group of Negotiators — and have consistently pushed the hardest at recent COPs for binding international commitments and dedicated drought financing, arguing their countries carry the least responsibility for the crisis but suffer disproportionately.
Saudi Arabia, which hosted COP16 in Riyadh in December 2024, has positioned itself as a leading voice on drought and dust storm early warning systems and has backed elements of the African position, even as it drew separate criticism at climate talks over its stance on fossil fuels.
China, one of the countries with the longest experience combating desertification through large-scale afforestation programs such as its "Great Green Wall" shelterbelt project, is regularly cited by the UNCCD as a source of technical expertise for other affected nations.
Mongolia, this year's host, has about 90% of its territory in arid, semi-arid, dry or sub-humid climatic zones vulnerable to desertification. Its vast grasslands are also central to the country's economy and way of life. The country has made land restoration a national priority, committing in 2024 to spend at least 1% of its GDP annually on fighting climate change and desertification. It has also promoted domestic initiatives such as its “Billion Trees” campaign and programs aimed at food security and sustainable land use.
In the previous summit held in Riyadh, negotiators launched the Riyadh Action Agenda to restore 1.5 billion hectares of land by 2030, established a $12.15 billion drought resilience alliance, and officially created caucuses to incorporate Indigenous Peoples and local communities into formal decision-making.
But the summit's most closely watched item — a proposed global framework, or possibly a legally binding protocol, for managing drought — ended without agreement.
The idea, championed mainly by the African bloc and small island states and backed by Saudi Arabia, was opposed by the US, the European Union, Australia and several Latin American countries over concerns about the cost.
The disagreement prevented an agreement at COP16, and the issue will return to the negotiating table at COP17.
Beyond drought, the Ulaanbaatar program is organized around finance, water, land, people and food systems.
UNCCD estimates that at least $2.6 trillion in investment is needed by 2030 to restore more than 1 billion hectares of degraded land and build drought resilience. COP17 will test whether previous pledges can translate into actual projects and financing.
Finally, coinciding with the UN International Year of Rangelands and Pastoralists, COP17 is expected to focus heavily on nomadic herding, livestock management, and dryland soil health. A ministerial dialogue on restoring rangelands and improving the well-being of pastoralist communities is scheduled for Aug. 26.

So Koizumi’s exhibition explores design through scent and space
Designer So Koizumi of So Koizumi Design presents his first solo exhibition in South Korea in collaboration with Seoul-based scent brand POINTTWOFIVE·SECOND. The exhibition takes place at 025S SEONGSU HAUS, the brand’s flagship space in Seoul, where Koizumi’s works are distributed throughout the interior as part of a spatial installation.
POINTTWOFIVE·SECOND is a scent brand centered on personal experiences of fragrance, memory, and emotion. Its approach extends beyond perfume to include tea, art, and other sensory experiences, creating a setting in which scent is considered alongside different forms of material and spatial engagement.
Material and sensory design shape So Koizumi’s Seoul exhibition
The exhibition by So Koizumi Design Studio brings together the designer’s ongoing research into the meanings embedded in materials and structures with POINTTWOFIVE·SECOND’s sensory approach. A selection of his works is positioned throughout the flagship space, establishing relationships between the objects, the existing interior, and the brand’s scent-based environment.
Rather than presenting the works as an isolated collection, the exhibition integrates them into the spatial setting of 025S SEONGSU HAUS. Their placement allows materials, structures, scent, and surrounding architectural elements to be experienced as connected components of the exhibition, creating a dialogue between Koizumi’s design practice and the atmosphere of the Seoul-based brand.
